1383. BENJAMIN HALL was indicted for stealing, on the 15th, of March, 12 knives, value 6 s.; and 12 forks, value 4 s.; the goods of John Sewell and another, his employers: and ANN HALL , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN SEWELL . I live in Fore-street, City, and am a jeweller. Benjamin Hall was my errand-boy for two or three years—he slept at his mother's—in consequence of some circumstances I went to her house with the officer, and found a great deal of my property—these knives and forks were at the pawnbroker's—the mother had not an opportunity of coming to my house and of taking these articles—I did not say a word to the boy about these knives and forks—these are them—they are new, and are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYYNE. Q. How do you know them; is there any private mark on them? A. No, I know them by seeing them on my shelves many times, and from their being a pecullar pattern—I cannot say when I last saw them.
COURT. Q. Are your knives wrapped up in this way, in such papers as these? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was there any appearance of poverty in the mother's lodging? A. I should say a great deal, though there were many things about the room that did not belong to them—the mantle shelf and table were full of China ornaments—up to this time the boy conducted himself pretty well.
COURT. Q. What did the woman say? A. That she had been very unhappy and miserable about them, and that she had warned her son not to
bring these things, and was several times coming to tell me—I said it was a pity she did not do so.
WILLIAM BOLTWOOD . I am assistant to Mr. Cotton, a pawnbroker, in Shoreditch. I produce these knives, which I took in on the 15th of March of the female prisoner, in the name of Ann Hall.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you know her before? A. Perfectly well—I had seen her a hundred times—she told me a young couple at her house had given 1 l. for them a short time before, and wanted to raise a few shillings on them—I do not know whether I told this to the Magistrate—I was merely asked whether I took them in—I never saw the boy.
(Samuel Avery, of Kensington, gave Ann Hall a good character.)
BENJAMIN HALL— GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
ANN HALL— GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoners.)
[trialAccount t18390408-1384]
1384. PATRICK M'CARTHY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 box, value 1 d.; 5 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, 1 half-crown, and 7 shillings; the goods and monies of John Mahoney, from the person of Mary Mahoney.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
MARY MAHONEY . I am the wife of John Mahoney. I was out with my husband on Saturday night, the 6th of April—I do not know whether the prisoner was an acquaintance of my husband's, but I have seen him, and we met him that night—my husband and he stood talking together in the street, and seemed very glad to see each other—the prisoner said, "Come, John, we will have a pint"—they went across to the corner of Moor-lane, and had a pint of porter—they stood talking some time, and the prisoner said, "We will go and sit down somewhere"—I had not drank any thing—they then went to the Stirling Castle in London-wall—I went in with them, and sat by the side of my husband—they had a pot of porter—after some time another pot of porter was called for, and then the prisoner called for half a quartern of the best "cream of the valley," that is gin—it was brought in a pewter measure, with a small glass—it remained there some time—the prisoner called the waiter, and told him to bring him a full measure—that is, a larger glass—the waiter brought it, and the prisoner poured what was in the measure into the large glass—it remained there some time, and the prisoner in the course of conversation with my husband kept fingering and handling the glass—he told my husband that he could earn 1 l. a-day, and he could lie in bed till twelve o'clock, and then turn out and earn 4 s., and I said to my husband, "How hard you have worked to bring me home 28 s."—the prisoner then said to me, "Here, Mrs. Mahoney, take this from me"—(holding me the glass of liquor)—I said, "No, thank you"—he held it up, and said, "This is a glass of good gin"—I refused it again, and my husband said to me, "It is cold, Mary, take it, it won't hurt you"—I took a little of it first, and then I finished it—in five minutes I felt my right hand drop down, and then my other hand, and I felt no support in my legs—my head fell back, and the gas-lights appeared to be blue and green—I could not tell any more—I fell back on my husband, and felt no more till I felt them putting me into a cab —(I remember hearing one word said about smuggling)—I was forced into a cab, and I remember somebody was sitting by my side in
the cab—I felt a hand at my right hand pocket—I thought it was my husband—it was a very rough hand, and I said' "John, don't handle my pocket, I feel so ill"—I had in my pocket five sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, one half-crown, and seven shillings—I had 5 l. 10 s. of my daughter's to save up, but I had taken sixpence of it at twelve o'clock to get dinner for my husband—the other money was my husband's wages—when I got home I was alone—the cab stopped very soon after the rough handling of my pocket, and the person who had been with me, was gone—when I got home I had no money to pay the cab, it was all gone, and my lodger paid for it.
COURT. Q. Had the prisoner had any conversation with your husband or you, so as to know that you had any money in your pocket? A. No more than my acknowledging that I had my husband's wages.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. What time did you go out? A. At ten o'clock, after supper—we intended to go to the Borough-market, but we went no further than Fore-street—I saw my husband speak to the prisoner once before that night-my husband gets his living by hard work as an iron-moulder—I do not know how the prisoner gets his living—our meeting that night was Quite by accident—I had never been in the gin-shop at the corner of Moor-lane, before—I had nothing there, but they had a pint of porter, no more—I had had nothing to drink that evening before I went out, but my tea.
Q. You say the prisoner kept fingering over the glass, did he put any thing into it? A. I had no suspicion of it then, but I am now well aware there must have been something put into it—I often take a glass of spirits, but I had not taken any that day till then—I cannot tell how long we remained at the Stirling Castle—we went there about eleven o'clock—we had not been an hour at the other public-house, we had stopped talking in the street some time—I am well aware I had my money when we went to the Stirling Castle—I do not know who called the cab.
JOHN MAHONEY . I am the witness's husband. I went out with her—we were going to the Borough—I met the prisoner in Fore-street, Cripplegate—we went to the corner of Moor-lane, and had a pint of after that to the Stirling Castle, in London-wall—we remained there till about twelve o'clock—we had one pot of beer first—the Prisoner called for some "cream of the valley," which was brought in a pewter measure, and a small glass which would contain half of it—after some time there was a large glass called for which would hold all the gin, and the Prisoner poured the—"cream of the valley" into the glass himself—it stood for a considerable time, while we were talking, right before him—I did not take notice whether his hand was near it, but the glass was near enough to him—after some time he asked my wife to drink it—she said would not—I invited her to do it again, and she did—after she drank it, she seemed motionless directly, and got sick in her stomach—I fetched a cab myself—the prisoner got into the cab first, and helped my wife in—I was then trying to get in, and the cab-man would not let me get in—I told the man to drive to my own place, and he drove off directly—when I got home my wife was a good deal better than when I put her into the cab —I did not know that she had any money of my daughter's to take care of—I never saw her so before after taking a glass of spirits.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did you go out? A. About ten o'clock—I had some gin at home—my wife did not take any, nor any porter—I had some gin at home-my wife did not take any, nor any porter—we
went first to the corner of Moor-lane—I do not know the name of the house—I may have been there before, once or twice—the prisoner and my wife went in with me, and there were a good many people there—we had one pint of porter there—there was no spirits drank by any of us—I cannot tell how long we staid there, nor at what time we got to the Stirling Castle, but we left about twelve o'clock, because they were shutting up—I think between him and me we had about three pots of beer at the Stirling Castle—my, wife was with us, but there were only ourselves where we sat—I never was in there in my life before—the prisoner did not take any "cream of the valley" himself to my knowledge—my wife took the whole—she then lost the use of all her limbs.
WILLIAM DYER . I am waiter at the Stirling Castle. On the Saturday night in Question, Mrs. Mahoney and her husband were there—the prisoner ordered the "cream of the valley," and I took it him in a pewter measure, with a small glass—after some time he called for a half-quartern glass—I took it to him—I saw him pour the gin out into it, and it stood by his side—I saw him give it to Mrs. Mahoney—she drank it in two drinks—in five or ten minutes after she was taken very ill—I did not see her leave, but the prisoner gave me a bundle when be left, and said, "Take care of this bundle, I will call for it in the morning"—he never called for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you opened the bundle? No, I believe there are straps in it—I could see them through the handkerchief—when they were at our house they had two pots of beer and a screw of tobacco—they had no other spirits of any kind.
PATRICK HICKEY . I live in the same house with Mr. and Mrs. Mahoney—I remember Mrs. Mahoney coming home on that Saturday night, about twelve o'clock—she was stupid, helpless, and drunk-like—I had never seen her in that state before—she is generally a sober Quiet woman—I took her out of the cab and into the house, and paid for the cab for her, as she had no money.
COURT. Q. What did Mrs. Mahoney say? A. She had her hand in her pocket, and she said all her money was gone and her child's money—she could neither stand nor move.
ELLEN DONOVAN . I am the prosecutor's daughter—I had saved up 5 l. 10 s., and gave it to my mother to keep for me.
WILLIAM HUGHES . I am a cab -man. I took Mrs. Mahoney from the corner of London-wall to Whitecross-street—there was a man with her in the cab, and her husband, as I have been given to understand, wanted to get in—I said I would carry more than two if they would pay me, and he said, "Then I will walk"—I do not know who the man was who was in the cab, but he went to the corner of Castle-court, and then got out—the husband had told me where to drive to—Mrs. Mahoney was in a state of exhaustion, and when the man left her he said, "This is the fruits of coming home with another man's wife—I do not know the house, now I will go and look for the husband," and away he went.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know who called the cab? A. No, I was paid by Mr. Hickey, the lodger—my fare was 8 d.—I said I would take more if they would give me 1 s.—Mr. Hickey gave me 1 s. after waiting a Quarter of an hour—I was told to drive to Castle-court, White-cross-street, but no number was given—I stopped at the end of Castle-court two or three minutes—I then called the policeman (as the man did not return) to know what I should do with the woman, and he went
up the court—Mr. Hickey came and took the woman—the policeman said he thought I ought to have a shilling.
SAMUEL EDGE ( City police-constable, No. 264.) I took the prisoner at the White Hart public-house, Bishopsgate-street, on the Sunday evening, at nine o'clock—I said, "I want you"—he said, "I did not take the woman's money"—I had not said any thing to him about money—this bundle was given to me at the station-house—it contains straps.
Cross-examined. Q. How did you know the prisoner was at the White Hart? A. The prosecutor told me where he was—he stood at the door and pointed him out.
COURT to JOHN MAHONEY. Q. Do you know where the prisoner lives? A. Yes, and I sent a messenger to him—he came out and said, "I will make all right what happened on Saturday night"—I was informed he lived in Still-alley—I went in the evening with the officer to the White Hart.
(George Gilbert, of Green Arbour-court, Old Bailey; Thomas Riley, a mechanic, of Baldwin's-gardens; Ann Russell; Ann Field, of Still-alley; John M'Nolly, of Cock-hill, a journeyman cheesemonger; and Ann Morris, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18390408-1385]
1385. BARNETT JOSEPHS was indicted for feloniously receiving of a certain evil-disposed person, on the 6th of March, 5001bs. weight of lead, value 30 s., the goods of William Miller, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.—2nd COUNT, calling it the goods of John Clay.—3rd COUNT, of the Right Honourable and Right Reverend Father in God, Charles James, Lord Bishop of London.—4th COUNT, of the Right Honourable and Most Reverend William, Lord Archbishop of Canterbury. —5th COUNT, of divers persons unknown.—6th COUNT, of certain persons unknown.
MR. PHILLIPS conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS RAWLEY . I am gate-keeper to Messrs. Pontifex, of Shoe-lane, they are coppersmiths. On the 6th of March the prisoner brought 40 cwt. and 12lbs. of lead—it was weighed—I saw a piece of it opened—it appeared to be coffin lead—a part of it is here now—some of the plates were cut out in my presence, and are here.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. How long have you been in the service of Messrs. Pontifex? A. Three years—I had been in the police, and left it for not doing my duty—I was not charged with being concerned in carrying on a private still—there was an illicit glass-work, and because I did not report it to my superior officer, I got discharged—the prisoner had dealt with Messrs. Pontifex before—I do not know wherher he buys metal of persons who go round the country to collect it in small Quantities—I examined it when it came in, I found nothing about it then—it was brought in one of Mr. Dale's carts, and put on a truck to be carried in—it was not taken off the truck—the prisoner came, and was there during the time of the truck's being loaded, and carried in—I was called down to see the marks on the lead in the last truck that was being unloaded—that might be about twenty minutes after the prisoner had left.
JOHN SHARP . I am foreman to Messrs. Pontifex. I saw this lead after it was discovered to be parts of coffins—I saw it the next day—I wrote to the prisoner about it—I afterwards saw him, and asked him how
he could account for the possession of this lead—he said he could not account for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say that he could not tell that it was his? A. Yes—he said he did not know that it was his, and if it was he did not know how he got it—I have been fourteen years with Messrs. Pontifex—the prisoner has frequently sold them lead and metal.
EDWARD HUTCHINGS ( city police-constable, No. 85.) I took the prisoner, and have the coffin-plates here, which have names on them.
( The inscriptions on some plates were here read, as follows)—"Ebenezer Hickley, died 29th June, 1831"—"John Holden, died 1817, aged 82 years"—"Sarah Graves, died 1834, aged 33 years."
THOMAS REECE . I am churchwarden of the parish of St. Leonard, Shoreditch, that is the church the vaults of which have been despoiled—I have seen these leaden plates—they belong to the coffins of persons who have been buried there—I find by the books that those persons were buried under the church—one is Ebenezer Hickley, 29th of June, 1831, and the others which have been produced.
WILLIAM MILLER . I was a relative of Sarah Graves—I buried her at my expense.
(Michael Hedges, a broker and general-dealer, in Millpond-street, Bermondsey; and Barnet Isaacs, of Hopkinson-street, Commercial-roiad, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY.—Aged 25.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18390408-1386]
1386. GEORGE JAMES GARRETT was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 2 handkerchiefs, value 8 s. 6 d., the goods of William Burchmore, his master.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM BURCHMORE . I am a linen-draper, carrying on business in Felix-terrace, Liverpool-road, Islington. On the 1st of December the prisoner came into my service as shop-boy—he was to have 8 l. a year—I had a character with him from Mr. Ludlow—he was not well furnished with clothes—on the 1st of January I paid him 13 s. 6 d. on, account of his wages, and advanced his salary 2 l. a year more—on Sunday, the 17th of March, I was in my kitchen—I looked into the safe in the area, where the prisoner kept his clothes, and found a pocket-book there with 6 s. 11 d. in it—I and my family kept a watch on him—I think it was on the 19th of March that I missed two handkerchiefs, which were taken from pieces which I have here—I asked the prisoner if they were sold—he said he had not sold them—on the 20th of March, when I came home at night, I sent him to bed in consequence of a communication made to me—I searched the window, and found the same pocket-book which I had seen before, in the window between two dresses—it then contained 23 s. 6 d. in silver, and 1 s. 51/2 d. in halfpence—Mr. Fowler called on me on the Sunday morning, and made a communication to me—I after that made another search, and found two silk handkerchiefs between the rafters of the floor and the skirting of the window, lying on the pavement—the shop-front runs out on the pavement, and near the skirting which goes on the pavement I found the hand-kerchiefs wrapped up—Mr. Fowler was with me when I found them—I put them back—they could not have got off the pieces by any accident—I had some difficulty to get, at them—I was obliged to go, down on my
hands and knees—Taylor, the policeman, came in the evening, and took the handkerchiefs—these are them—they belong to these pieces.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have there been any instances in which a customer, after things have been cut off, has declined to buy them? A. Never in my house—I have known it in other houses—I am not always at home—I raised his salary because he suited me, and I wished to give him encouragement—I did not raise it on. account of his good conduct, certainly—I never swore that I did, to my knowledge.
Q. Is this true or false, "I raised his salary in consequence of good conduct?" A. It is not true, sir, neither did I ever state that I did, to my knowledge—I do not remember it—I decline saying positively whether I did or not—I took him on suspicion of taking money from my till, which I found deposited, as I have stated, in my window—Mr. Fowler afterwards came to me on the Sunday morning, and asked me if I had missed any handkerchiefs—I will swear I never charged the prisoner with embezzlement—I never swore so—I merely stated that my suspicions were that be must have put the handkerchiefs where I found them—I never said, after charging him with embezzlement, that I found I was wrong.
Q. Now attend to this Question, for it behoves you—did you ever say this to any body, that you would get the prisoner sent to Newgate to prevent an action being brought, and then at the Old Bailey you would try to get him off? A. I said to Mr. Ludlow, "The parents have threatened me with an action, I shall be compelled to go on now"—I did not wish to prosecute the boy—I never said I found I was wrong in the charge—I never said I first charged him with embezzlement, and found I was wrong in the charge—I went to Mr. Ludlow to ask the prisoner's previous character after I had been told about the handkerchiefs, but I did not mention about the handkerchiefs to him—the prisoner was not held to bail on a charge of embezzlement, that I swear.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did you charge him on suspicion of robbing your till? A. Yes—that was the charge before the Justice—that was after I found the pocket-book with the increase of the money—he was remanded on bail on the Monday—I was before the Justice on the Friday, and he was remanded till the Monday—and on the Sunday prior to that Monday Mr. Fowler came to me, and in consequence of what he said to me I made this charge about the handkerchiefs when I went again on Monday.
SAMUEL HALE FOWLER . I live at No. 5, Felix-terrace, Liverpool-road. In consequence of a communication which my son made to me, I went on the Sunday to Mr. Burchmore's, and in consequence of the communication of my son, Mr. Burchmore searched a particular place in his shop which I described—I saw him find the two handkerchiefs.
JAMES FOWLER . I shall be fifteen years old next Friday—I am the son of the last witness—I know the prisoner, and I know a boy named Jones. On Friday, the 22nd of March, about six o'clock in the evening, Jones and I were walking by Westminster-bridge—the prisoner joined us, and told us to keep out of the way till after Monday, as he said he should get clear—he said he had had two silk handkerchiefs in his jacket, and he had placed them under the shop window—I told my father of it on Saturday, the next day.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you run away from your father? A. Yes, I did—I have told all the conversation that took place between the prisoner and me—I am sure I have forgotten nothing remarkable—he, told me he
had two silk handkerchiefs in his jacket pocket, and he placed them under the window—he said nothing else—I did not know that Jones had robbed his own father—I never swore that I did.
Q. Did not the Magistrate say he bailed him (the prisoner) because he had a doubt whether you were telling the truth or not, as you admitted you went away with a boy that robbed his own father, and you knew it? A. No—I never said to a gentleman that I went away with Jones, and I knew he robbed his father of 13 s. 6 d.—Jones was before the Magistrate.
RICHARD PIPPIN JONES . I was with the last witness and the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you and Fowler go away that night? A. Yes—I was suspected of robbing my father—I told that to Fowler, and he knew it when he went away with me—I was at Hatton-garden—I remember Fowler being sworn there—he told the Magistrate there he went away with me, and that he knew I had robbed my father of 13 s. 6 d.
SAMUEL TAYLOR ( police-constable N 167.) I was sent for to the prosecutor's on the Sunday evening, and took the prisoner—I have the hand-kerchiefs.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not the prisoner taken first on a charge of embezzlement? A. Yes, by his master.
MR. PHILLIPS called DAVID LUDLOW. I live in Crawford-street. On Sunday, the 24th of March, the prosecutor called on me—he inquired about the prisoner's character—he did not say one word about his having a charge against him for stealing handkerchiefs—he told me at the office that he would get the prisoner sent to Newgate, to prevent an action being brought; and that when he got him there he would try to get him off.
MR. DOANE. Q. I believe the prosecutor had a character of the prisoner from you? A. Yes, he had—when he came to me on the Sunday, be came to know whether I had any suspicion of the prisoner, and said he had robbed him of 13 s. 6 d.—my wife and my wife's mother were present—he did not say that as they had threatened to bring an action against him, be must go on with the prosecution—I said, "Has not he robbed you of any goods?"—he said, "No, I am sorry to say he has not, because the most distressing thing of all is, the boy, who he says, gave him the money, has gone away, and his parents are distressed, as he has not returned"—he said to me, at the police-office, "It may appear harsh to you to go on in this case, but, unless I get the boy convicted, I shall be liable to an action."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1387]
1387. ELIZABATH MURPHY was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 tongue, value 8 d., the goods of Daniel Lucas.
DANIEL LUCAS . I live in Calmel-buildings, and am a grocer. About twelve o'clock on Saturday night, the 6th of April, the prisoner came to purchase some tea and sugar—she paid, and was about to leave the shop—she then said she had lost a thimble—my young man took the candle to go and look by the counter for it—I saw the prisoner take a tongue, and put it into her apron—I said, "What have you got in your apron?"—she said, nothing but what she had paid me for—I took down her apron, and found this tongue—she got outside—I called the officer, and gave her in charge.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you know her before? A. I had—she was not intoxicated.
GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Nine Days.

1388. HENRY EMERY was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of March, 1 coat, value 2 l. 10 s., the goods of Thomas Danforth.
THOMAS DANFORTH . I live in Back-road, St. George's East The prisoner lived in the same house, but not in the same room—I had a coat on the 30th of March—I missed it on the 31st, when I went to my box—this is it.—( looking at it)—I had a letter sent to me on Easter Monday, with the duplicate in it.
JAMES GILL . I am shopman to Mr. Fleming, a pawnbroker. I took in this coat of the prisoner, to the best of my belief.
THOMAS SQUIRE ( police-constable K 182.) I took the prisoner—I told him it was for stealing a coat—he said, "You must prove it"—I said, "It is pawned in the name of Smith"—he said, "Smith knows nothing about it; Green took it, and I pawned it"—I said, "The prosecutor has received a letter with the duplicate, is it your writing?"—he said, "Yes, I sent it."
Prisoner's Defence. I took the coat as a pattern round to the trade—Green went with me—I stood outside, while he took it into shops, and then he went away with it—the letter I never wrote nor sent—I never pawned the coat.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Two Months.

1389. AMELIA READER was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 counterpane, value 3 s., and 1 pair of stays, value 3 s., the goods of John Reader: and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN READER . I live in Princes-street, Tyson-street, Bethnal-green—I left home on the 9th of April, about ten o'clock in the morning, and left my daughter (the prisoner) in possession of my house—at eleven o'clock in the evening I came home, and missed ray counterpane off my bed, and the following day I missed the stays—the prisoner came home, I told her I had missed them—she acknowledged taking them, and said she had pledged them—I have no wife, I have three other small children—the prisoner is the eldest—she said she wished to be transported for seven years.
WILLIAM GOLDER . I am a pawnbroker. I produce the counterpane and stays, which I took in of the prisoner.
Prisoner. I am very sorry for what I have done—I will not do so any more—it is through my sister, who turned me out of doors—my father has more need to correct his other children than me—he has said he wished to transport me—I want to go home to work to earn money.
CHARLES BYRON ( police-constable H 157.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction from Mr. Clark's office—(read)—the prisoner is the person who was tried.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years,—Penitentiary.

1390. BENJAMIN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 1 jacket, value 4 s., the goods of George Webster.
GEORGE WEBSTER . I am servant to Mr. Malcolm, a surgeon in Ratcliffe-highway—I had a jacket on the 7th of April, hanging by the parlour-door—I missed it about nine o'clock—this is it.
GEORGE MALCOLM . I am a surgeon in Ratcliffe-highway—about half-past nine o'clock that evening, I saw the prisoner coming out of my passage,
where this jacket was—I asked him what he wanted, he said "A penny worth of liquorice-pipe"—I saw he had this jacket under his arm—he told me his brother bad picked it up across the street, and had given it to him but it had been hanging up in my house—I saw another boy standing at my door when I went in.
PATRICK HORAN ( police-constable H 176.) I received the prisoner in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. My brother gave me the jacket—I went into the shop, and the gentleman took me—I said I would get my brother, I went out three yards, and then came back with the jacket under my arm.
MR. MALCOLM re-examined. He did not leave the house at all.
GUILTY. Aged 13.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Ten Days, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18390408-1391]
1391. RACHEL HACKET and WILLIAM GLADMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 6 sovereigns, the monies of William Richmond, from his person.
WILLIAM RICHMOND . I am a brick-maker, residing at North Hyde, in Middlesex—on the 8th of April, between nine and ten o'clock, I was at a public-house at Hanwell—the two prisoners were there and another person with them—I went in to have a pint of beer—I had taken a £10 note from home and changed it—I had seven sovereigns with me and some silver—Hacket forced herself into my company—she came and sat along-side of me, and put her hand into my breeches—she said she would have it, whether or nay—I said, "Keep off, I don't want any thing of the kind"—we had a drop of beer together—I cannot say that I was exactly sober—I was rather fresh —I know she pulled me and mauled me about a good deal—after that, I put my hand into my pocket, and missed six sovereigns besides some silver—no other person could have taken it—she and the two men were gone then.
Hacket. Q. Did you not send me for some snuff, and give me some money? A. No; not a halfpenny—when I paid for a pint of beer, she saw the sovereigns in my hand, and she wanted to pick a few halfpence out from among them.
CHARLES SWEETMAN . I live at the public-house. The two prisoners came there together—the prosecutor sat opposite to Gladman, and Hacket sat by his side—I saw her take very great liberties with him, and her hand was either in his small-clothes or his pocket—I suppose the prisoners went out first, but I did not see them go—the prosecutor did not state his loss that night—I did not hear he had been robbed till the next morning—he staid at our house waiting for the arrival of his wife—he went home with his wife—he paid for what he had.
ROBERT RICHMOND . I am the prosecutor's son. He told me of his loss—on the Tuesday morning I went to Hanwell, and saw Gladman and an old man drinking with him—the men were pointing to Gladman about a pair of new shoes he had on, and a smock frock—he put up his leg, and said, "I should not have had them, if she had not worked hard last night for them."
JAMES CUISHEN ( police-constable T 135.) I received information of this on the Tuesday, and about an hour afterwards I saw the two prisoners in a public-house in New Brentford—I called Hacket out, and told her I must take her, for robbing a man of seven sovereigns and a purse—she
said she knew nothing about it, she had not been with any man at all—I then called Gladman out—he said he knew nothing of it—in going along I asked Hacket if she had been to, the pawnbroker's—the said the had not—I then asked Gladman how he came by the new boots and the smock-frock—he said he bought them with money that she (Hacket) had given him.
JOHM WILLIAM JENNINGS . I am a pawnbroker, and live at Brentford. On that Tuesday morning Hacket came and redeemed two pledges for 12 s.—she bought a gown for 8 s., and gave me a sovereign.
Hacket. The prosecutor went out with me, and gave me some money, and the next morning I asked this prisoner, who is my brother, to accompany me to Brentford—the prosecutor told the Magistrate he had 800 l. in his pocket.
WILLIAM RICHMOND re-examined. I went home to bed at ten o'clock—I had better than 1100 l. in my side pocket, in bills.
HACKET— GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Six Months.
GLADMAN— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1392]
1392. WILLIAM MAYBIN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 1 purse, value 2 d.; 1 half-crown, 2 shillings, 4 pence, and 6 halfpence; the goods and monies of John Day, from the person of, Hannah Day.
HANNAH DAY . I am the wife of George Day, of Denmark-street, St. George's-in-the-East. About ten o'clock in the evening of the 9th of April, I was standing at a picture shop—I saw a little boy by my side, and a boy behind me—I had a little child with me—I lifted her up to show her the pictures—I had 5 s. and a penny in all in my pocket—I had one half-crown and 2 s.—I felt my pocket robbed, and I heard the other boy say to the prisoner, "Come on, it is all right"—there were others about, but no one could have taken my money but those two—they both ran off together—I ran, till I fell down, and could not run any further—this is my purse and money—( looking at them.)
DANIEL BURGESS . I saw the prisoner going along the street, and the prosecutrix running after him, calling, "Stop thief"—I stopped him—he said, "I have got nothing," and as soon as he made use of those words he dropped some coppers down an area—I did not see the purse found—I took the prisoner to the station-house.
HONOBA CARTHY . I saw two boys running, and the prosecutrix was crying, "Stop thief"—Burgess stopped one of the boys, and at that time one of them threw this purse down—I ran and took it up, and gave it to the policeman.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw a boy and a woman running, calling, "Stop thief"—I did not throw any coppers away.
(The prisoner received a. good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month; the last Week Solitary.
[trialAccount t18390408-1393]
1393. ELIZA SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 pair of pattens, value 1 s., the goods of William Adams.
WILLIAM ADAMS . I am a pawnbroker, and live in High-street, Shoreditch. On the 6th of April the prisoner came to transact some business, about half-past seven o'clock, and as she left the shop she stooped down,
put her hand under a table, and took a pair of pattens from a lot—I followed, and brought her back.
LAMBERT WILKINSON ( police-constable G 175.) I took the prisoner and have the pattens.
Prisoner. A person with me took them, and gave them to me.
WILLIAM ADAMS re-examined. There was a woman with her, who walked before her, and as the prisoner followed her she stooped and took them.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1394]
1394. MARY M'GILL was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 10 handkerchiefs, value 2 l., the goods of William Dalton, her master.
WILLIAM DALTON . I live in Harper-street, Chelsea. The prisoner worked for me on the 5th of April—I heard from Lady Ross and others that their things were not right—I then went to the pawnbroker's, and found four handkerchiefs—I got an officer—I went to the prisoner's house, and gave her in charge—these handkerchiefs are mine—they belonged to my customers—I had them to wash.
GEORGE BROOKS . I am a pawnbroker. I produce seven handkerchiefs which I took in of a woman at different times, in the name of Ann Clarkson—these are the duplicates I gave—I do not know the woman.
HENRY KIMBER ( police-sergeant V 20.) I went to the prisoner—she said she knew nothing about them—I said I was going to take her to the station-house—she asked me to allow her to put on another gown—she then said if the prosecutor would allow, her time she would take them out.
WILLIAM DALTON re-examined. The man the prisoner lives with, said in her presence, that her name was Clarkson, but she always worked for me in the name of M'Gill.
Prisoner. I did not pledge all the handkerchiefs—I throw myself on the mercy of the Court.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18390408-1395]
1395. JAMES HOLSTON was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 2 yards of woollen cloth, value 2 l. 2 s., the goods of Vincent Daniels.
VINCENT DANIELS . I live in Brewer-street, and am a woollen draper. On the 9th of April, the prisoner came in with two patterns of silk, and asked if I could match them—I said I could not—he then untied a piece of, cloth, and asked if I could match that—I said I could not—he asked me to show him the nearest I had, and while I was getting that, there was a remnant of cloth on the counter, which he tied up—he said the cloth I showed him was not near enough, and was going out—when he got to the door, I asked him to walk in again—he came in and said, "For God's sake Mr. Daniels don't do it, I will give you it back."
Prisoner. Q. Do you state that I took the cloth, tied it in my hand-kerchief, and went to the door? A. Yes, I collared you at the door—a young man came to the door, and I sent him for the policeman—the prisoner said, "No, I will pay Mr. Daniels for it"—he ran behind the counter, untied the cloth and his own garment, threw it under the counter, and said, "What have I got of yours?"—he had no money about him, only a flash note—when the policeman came, he was in the middle of the shop—I had hold of his arm—he had got out of the house when I took him back—when the policeman came I fetched this piece of, cloth from
under the counter—I did not select it from a number of pieces on the counter.
COURT. Q. Do you swear that he tied it up in his handkerchief, and took it out of the door? A. Yes, he got down three steps—he tied up his little coat first, then opened one corner, and tied up this cloth.
JOHN MURCH ( police-constable C 106.) I was sent for—when I got to the shop, the prosecutor was holding the prisoner by the arm—he said he had taken a piece of cloth—Mr. Daniels went behind the counter and picked up the cloth—I searched the prisoner at the station-house, and found no money on him, but a pocket-book, and a flash note.
Prisoner. I said I had a half-sovereign and a half-crown. Witness. No, you did not.
Prisoner's Defence. I had a little portion of a tunic coat in the bundle, and I wanted to make a pair of towsers to match it for my own child, and to match some waistcoats—he said he had not got it—my little coat was then lying on the pieces of cloth. I took the coat and tied it up in my bundle—he was looking at me all the time—it is not likely I should take any thing else—I had not stepped beyond the threshold of the door—he took me and told a young man to fetch a policeman—I said, "For what?"—he said. "If you don't know what is your own, I will tell you"—I said, "I have nothing but what I paid for"—I did not say, "I will pay you"—he selected this from a quantity of pieces, and I said, "If you are so inclined, why not take the first piece that comes to hand?"—that note was put into my hand one day in the street.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined One Year.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18390408-1396]
1396. WILLIAM HODGES was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of March, 1 saddle, value 5 s., the goods of Moses Jacobs, his master.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
STEPHEN SLAUGHTER . I am in the employ of Moses Jacobs, a glass-merchant, in Berwick-street, Oxford-street. The prisoner was occasionally employed as a labourer—my master rents some premises in Rupert-street, where the horse and cart are usually put up—on the 16th of March, the prisoner was employed to move the cart and some harness from one stable to another, in Rupert-street, Haymarket—I saw a gig saddle and other things safe in the stable, between six and seven o'clock, when I went to do up my horse—I went there again the next morning, and they were gone—on the 22nd I came through Smithfield, and saw a lad with my master's saddle for sale—I knew it, and gave the lad into custody—this is the saddle—I never authorised any body to take it.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. What name is over Mr. Jacobs' door? A. Jacobs only—his son's name David Jacobs, it is on the cart, but it is his father's business.
DANIEL SUTHERWOOD . I am a costermonger. I bought this saddle of the prisoner on Saturday night, three or four weeks back, for 1 s. 6 d.—I sent it to Smithfield on the Friday following, to be sold by a boy—it would not fit my donkey—it has been a pony-saddle—he said he had a collar and harness to sell.
Cross-examined. Q. Is the boy here? A. No—I know it is the saddle I bought—I gave it to a boy named Johnny —he works for me.
THOMAS WELLS ( police-sergeant C 1.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 4th of April, at a house in Hopton-street, where he was wakeing a dead body—I had been trying to find him for several days—I asked if he knew Mr. Jacobs wanted him for stealing some harness—he said he did, but he was advised to keep out of the way.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say his master's son advised him to keep out? A. He might—he said at the office that the son gave it him.
DAVID JACOBS . I am the son of Moses Jacobs. I never gave this saddle or any harness to the prisoner—I never authorised him to take it from the stable.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it your saddle? A. No—the cart is not mine—my name is on it, but it was bought with my father's money—my father's name is on the shop, not David—the name over the door is Jacobs—the horse which this harness was bought for, was sold, but when we bought another horse, we put this saddle on it—I do not know what chucking things to blazes means—I have not been attacked by my father for giving these things away—I never sent the prisoner a message—I did not know where he was to be found.
MOSES JACOBS . I keep a glass-shop in Berwick-street. I have been a housekeeper twenty-five years—I did not authorise the prisoner or any one to take away this harness or saddle—it belonged to me, only the name of Jacobs is over the door—I never charged a man with giving away these things.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1397]
1397. GEORGE THOMSON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 1 shawl, value 3 s., the goods of Ellen Paine, from her person.
ELLEN PAINE . On the 12th of April I met the prisoner and another young man in a public-house in North-street, Knightsbridge—I had two shawls on—I missed one in a house which the prisoner and another man, named Butcher, brought me to—they were strangers to me—I am an unfortunate girl—I had the shawl loose on me, and as the prisoner was going out of the room, he took it, and put the light out as he was going out—I said to the other young man that I had missed my shawl—he said it must be about the room—he got a light and looked about the room, and it was not to be found—Butcher and I came out of the house—I gave Butcher in charge as I came out, and he told where Thomson lived—this is my shawl—( looking at it.)
WILLIAM WARDLOW ( police-constable B 87.) The prosecutrix accused Butcher of stealing the shawl—I took him to the station-house—he then said it was the other person—that his name was Thomson, and directed me where to go—I went to No. 12, Kingston-street, Pimlico, and found the prisoner and the shawl—he said he knew nothing about it.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe the prisoner opened the door? A. Yes.
MR. PHILLIPS called
— BUTCHER. I was with the prisoner's brother last Thursday night at the public-house, and with the prosecutrix.
JOSEPH THOMPSON . On Thursday night I was at a public-house with Butcher—I went to a house with the prosecutrix—I had a travelling shawl about my neck—I was rather tipsy—I put the shawl into my pocket—the
lights were put out when I got up from the bed, and felt for the shawl, I found a shawl, and put it round my neck, and went home—I was at home when the policeman came to my house—they took my brother, the prisoner—my mother let me in that night—the policeman came to the door to me while I was throwing some dirt up at the windows, and spoke to me—he had an opportunity of seeing me.
HANNAH THOMSON . On Thursday night I let my son Joseph into my house—he appeared to me to be tipsy—he stumbled on the stain—I helped him up—I did not observe whether he had a shawl on—our door is dark, having an arch-way over it—my son George, the prisoner, was in bed at eleven o'clock that night.
GEORGE LARKIN ( police-constable B 65.) I remember on Friday morning seeing Joseph Thomson throw dirt up at the window—I went to him and told him to be careful not to break the windows.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1398]
1398. ELIZA M'CARTHY was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of April, 7 yards of calico, value 4 s.; 2 shirts, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 gown, value 3 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, value 5 s. 6 d.; 1 sheet, value 3 s.; 1 table-cloth, value 5 s.; 1 waistcoat, value 1 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 12 s.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 1 s.; and a two-penny piece; the goods and monies of Samuel Blain.
CHARLOTTE BLAIN . I am the wife of Samuel Blain, we lodge in a first-floor in Devonshire-street, at a coffee-shop—the prisoner used frequently to come there of a morning for about ten minutes, to have her coffee—she was there on the afternoon of the 3rd of April—I saw her go into the yard—I was in the back parlour—my room door was shut when I left it, but not locked—my property was safe—I was absent about an hour and a half, and when I returned I found the room stripped of all the property—the two-penny piece had been in a little box in the drawer that the clothes were taken out of—it was a two-penny copper-piece—I saw the prisoner again the same evening at the station-house, about two hours after I lost my property—I have lost all my property except the two-penny piece—it was not locked in the drawer, but the little box was—I had seen her at the shop that evening.
WILLIAM FINCH . I can swear to this two-penny piece by the marks on it—I had sold it to Samuel Blain nine or ten months ago—I am certain it is the same.
THOMAS HENRY THOMSON ( police-sergeant P 4.) I took the prisoner—she denied being in Devonshire-street at all or at the coffee-shop—I asked if she did not know a shop there—she said, "No," and she had not been there—I found this two-penny piece on her, 17 s. in silver, 31/2 d. in copper, a token, and four keys, one of which opens the private door of the house the prosecutor lodges in.
ELIZABETH SPREADBOROUGH . I searched the prisoner, and found this property on her.
CHARLOTTE BLAIN re-examined. This is the two-penny piece my husband had and lost.
SAMUEL BLAIN . I bought this two-penny piece of Finch—I believe this to be the same—it was in this box—it was broken open and the Piece taken out.
Prisoner's Defence. The two-penny piece was my own, and the money too.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.

1399. JOHN THOMSON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of March, 1 shovel, value 2 s., the goods of William Butcher.
WILLIAM BUTCHER . About the 1st of March, I was at work at Mr. Hutchings's, at Kensington, in the garden—I left my shovel in Mr. Hutchings's shed—I did not see it again till the 6th of April—the prisoner was at work there.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How do you know it? A. There are some letters on the handle which I can see plainly.
JOHN SLAUGHTER . I went into Mr. Taylor's, opposite Mr. Hutchings's, a fortnight ago last Wednesday—I asked the prisoner if he knew any body that could lend me a shovel—he said, "I have got one—I gave 1 s. and a pot of beer for"—I said, "I will give you 1 s. and a pint of beer for it"—I gave him 6 d., and the next night he brought it openly before the people, and I paid him.
NOT GUILTY .

1400. MARY WATSON was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 decanter, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Frederick Hardy.
FREDERICK HARDT . I live in York-street, Westminster, and am an earthenware dealer. On the 8th of April I saw the prisoner come between eight and nine feet from the pavement into the shop, which has an open front—she took this decanter, put it under her shawl, and walked away—I went and took it from her two doors off.
JOHN SMITHERS . I am a police-constable. I took the prisoner, and found nothing on her.
(The prisoner pleaded poverty.)

1401. CHARLES POULTON and JOHN SMITH were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 2 half-crowns and 1 shilling, the monies of James Simpson, from his person.
JAMES SIMPSON . I live with my parents—my father is an appraiser, in Thornhill-street, Islington. I was playing with some boys on the, 9th of April, in the Chalk-road—I got my hands dirty, and washed them in some water in the road—in pulling out my handkerchief to dry them, I pulled out two half-crowns and two shillings, and they dropped into the rough road—I picked up the half-crowns and one of the shillings, and was looking for the other, when the prisoners came up, and Poulton asked me how much I had lost—I told him what I had picked up, and that I was looking for another shilling—he asked me to show him what I had found, and I showed it him in my hand—Smith was there at the time, and began searching one of the boys—Poulton then took the two half-crowns out of my hand, and knocked the shilling out of my hand—Poulton said, "I will have four"—Smith took up the shilling and put it into his pocket—they ran off—the boys followed them down the Chalk-road, and called "Stop thief"—Poulton threw stones at them—a gentleman assisted us, and went
for a policeman—when the gentleman was gone for the policeman, Poulton gave me one half-crown, and said that was the half-crown he had taken from me—when Smith took my shilling—I took him by the collar and held him, and said I would have it—he pushed me and said he had not got it—that he bad got three shillings from the Gas Company, but he had not got my shilling, but I am quite sure he had it—the policeman came and took them both.
JOHN BRIGHTMAN . I was playing with the prosecutor—in pulling out hit handkerchief he pulled out two half-crowns and two shllings—he found the two half-crowns and one of the shillings—Poulton took the two half-crowns, and Smith picked up the shilling when it fell from hit hand—the prosecutor seized Smith by the collar—I saw Poulton give the prosecutor one half-crown, and when he was taken, another half-crown was found on him.
JAMES COLLETT . I was at play. What has been stated is correct—when the prisoners ran away, we boys pursued them, and Poulton threw stones at us—a gentleman on horseback assisted us, and went for a policeman.
JOSEPH YOUNG . I am a policeman. I was called, and went up to the prisoners—the prosecutor said they had robbed him of two half-crowns, and a shilling—Poulton said, "I only took one half-crown, which I have given him again"—I took them both to the station-house—I found 3 s. on Smith, which he at first said was his own—he afterwards said 1 s. belonged to the boy, which he was willing to give him—I found one half-crown on Poulton.
Poulton. That half-crown and 21/2 d., belonged to me—I took but one half-crown, and that I gave him again.
(Poulton received a good character.)
POULTON— GUILTY . Aged 19.
SMITH— GUILTY . Aged 23.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1402]
1402. CORNELIUS CONNELL was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 4lbs. weight of lead, value 6 d., the goods of Henry Collin Johnson, and fixed to a building; against the Statute, &c.
PAUL KINGSTON . I am in the employ of Mr. Johnson, a scavenger—the Blackwall rail-road crosses his yard—I saw the prisoner there with a pick-axe on his shoulder—he went to the leaden pipe, which if in the yard, struck it on each side, cut it off, put it under his smock-frock, and went away—I spoke to the policeman about it.
WILLIAM TAPLIN . I am a policeman. Kingston came and told me of this—I saw the prisoner coming out of a marine-store shop, about a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's—I went in, and inquired—I pursued, and overtook him—I said, "Where is that lead pipe?"—I put my hand on his breast, and found it in his smock-frock.
Prisoner. I told you I picked it up in the railway—I found it cut there, and took it.
COURT. Q. Did you fit the lead to the place? A. Yes, and it appeared to have been cut with some sharp instrument—it could not have been done with the pick-axe, because both the ends of that are pointed.
WILLIAM SHAW . I heard the prisoner say the lead belonged to the railway—I held him while the other officer went for the prosecutor—the prisoner asked me to let him go, and keep the pipe.
HENRY COLLIN JOHNSON . This lead is my property. The pipe goes
from my father's house to mine, through my yard—it was covered over and he must have dug three feet to get it—the prisoner had no business in my yard—I have seen him work in the gas-works opposite me.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to work, and met with this bit of pipe in the railway, and I took it up.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1403]
1403. ELIZABETH COLCOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of March, 2 sovereigns, the monies of James Rayner.
MARY RAYNER . I am the wife of James Rayner, of Nelson-street, Bethnal-green. On the 15th of March I asked the prisoner's parents to let her come and stay with my children while I went to the doctor's, which she did—I had in my drawer a purse containing nine sovereigns—I left the key in the drawer, and when I came back, I sent the prisoner home—on that day week I missed 2 sovereigns—I went to the prisoner at her mother's, and asked her if she had taken them—I said it might be better for her if she owned to it—no one else could have taken it.
WILLIAM PRICE . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner—I asked her if she knew the difference between a sovereign and a shilling—she said she did, and then she told me, voluntarily, that she took the two sovereigns home to her mother.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1404]
1404. GEORGE BEDFORD was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 1 coat, value 4 l., the goods of John Stewart.
WILLIAM UPTON . I am in the employ of Mr. John Stewart, of Cheap-side. On the 12th of April I had a coat thrown across the block in the shop—I saw the prisoner, about eight o'clock in the evening, walk into the shop, take it off the block, and walk out immediately—he ran and I ran, and called "Stop thief"—I never lost sight of him—he was stopped in Honey-lane-market—he dropped the coat—it was taken up and given to me—this is it—I am sure the prisoner is the person who took it.
Prisoner's Defence. I was returning home that day, and seeing the coat, I thought I could make a trifle of it, to make up my things, which I had lost—I was half groggy at the time.
GUILTY .— Confined One Month.
OLD COURT.— Wednesday, April 17 th, 1839.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arobin.
[trialAccount t18390408-1405]
1405. BENJAMIN MICHAEL was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 1 box, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 gown, value 1 l. 5 s.; 1 shawl, value 1 l. 5 s.; 7 yards of printed cotton, value 7 s.; 1 cloak, value 10 s.; 3 frocks, value 5 s.; 1 cape, value 8 s.; part of a weighing-machine, value 5 s.; 4 pinafores, value 10 s.; 1 veil, value 30 s.; 2 dishes, value 2 s.; 60 plates, value 2 s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 5 s.; the goods of Elizabeth Smith; and 1 coat, value 3 l. 10 s., and 1 waistcoat, value 15 s.; the goods of John Rushby.
RICHARD LEE . I am a potato-salesman, in Spitalfields market. Between seven and eight o'clock, on the 12th of April, I was opposite the Mint, and as I entered the Minories I saw a van, and the prisoner walking behind it, trying to get a box from it—I suspected him, as the van was going along, and
watched him—he kept trying to get it at different turnings, and opposite Rosemary-lane he tried again, without effect—opposite the Mint he took the box out of the tail of the van—I let him go about ten yards with it—I ran immediately, caught hold of him, and charged him with stealing it from the van—he said, "You did not see me"—I called the policeman, who came and took him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far did you watch the man? A. From the top of the Minories at Aldgate, up to the Mint—I have tried to get into the City Police, my father being a liveryman—I did not succeed, on account of my height—I was never accused of any thing wrong—I know Esther Haynes—I was not employed to whitewash a room of hers—she never accused me of taking 1 l. 5 s. out of her drawer—I know Charlotte Lawton—I never pretended to her that I was a widower, that my wife was buried in Stepney-churchyard, and want her to marry me—I did not show her my wife's grave—she came after me—my wife was living at the time, and is now—Lawton lent me two sovereigns—I paid her again, and have the receipt to prove it.
Q. Why not stop the man before, and not wait till he took the box; why not tell the carman somebody was trying to rob him? A. I cannot tell my reason—the box was very heavy, he could hardly carry it—I will take my oath he had it on his hip.
JOHN RUSHBY . I am a builder, and live in Lincolnshire. I was with my van, the carman was driving it, who I employed to take the goods to the wharf—I was called to stop, which I did, and missed the box—I saw the prisoner in custody of a policeman, and the box was produced—there was a coat of my own in it, and the other property belonged to my sister, Elizabeth Smith.
Cross-examined. Q. What sort of a van was it? A. A four-wheeled van—the box was at the back part of it—not fastened—it could not have fallen out, for it was too far in, and the tail-board was up.
JAMES SMITH . I am a policeman. I heard the cry of "Policeman," and saw Lee holding the prisoner in one hand and the box in the other—he charged him with taking it—the prisoner immediately said he did not take it, that it fell from the van and he picked it up.
(Ephraim Jacobs, Pastrycook, Old Castle-Street, Whitechapel;——Isaacs, furrier, Middlesex-street, Whitechapel; and Stephen Baylis, hair-dresser, Middlesex-street, Aldgate, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.

1408. ROBERT PHILPOT was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying John Childs.
CHARLES M'EROY . I am policeman. On the 6th of March, between
eleven and twelve o'clock at night, I was on duty in Aldersgate-street—I went to the Castle and Falcon tap-room—there was some altercation there—there were thirteen or fourteen individuals in the room, and a woman among them—John Childs, the deceased, was there, and the prisoner, who is the landlord of the house—they were wishing him to stand half a gallon of beer—he said he would not, and he would not draw any more beer—he then took some pots off the table, went to the bar, closed the door, and put the light out in the bar—he then came into the tap-room, laid hold of the table which I was leaning against, and threw the pots off on to the floor—he was the worse for liquor—he was gathering the pots off the ground, and had some of them in his hand when I came out of the tap-room—as I came out I saw the deceased with his arm up towards the gaslight, and as I passed out at the door I saw the light go out—I have no doubt he turned the gas off—as I passed out of the passage the deceased passed me, running and laughing, and the prisoner followed him with a quart pot in his right hand, and three pint pots in his left hand—they passed me up the yard, and I saw the prisoner strike the deceased first with the quart pot on the back—he then made a hit at him with his left hand with the three pots, which missed him—the deceased turned round to look behind him, and the prisoner with his right hand struck him on the left ear with the quart pot that he held in that hand—the deceased then cried out "Don't murder me," and was in the act of stooping to pick up his hat, which had fallen off, when the prisoner made another blow at him with the right hand, and seeing a scuffle between them I ran up—he picked up his hat—the prisoner was aiming another blow at him with his right hand when I jumped in between them and received the blow on my right shoulder—he made another blow with his left hand which I caught on my breast—I got hold of him by the arm, and he struggled several times more to get at him, which I prevented—I had not heard the deceased say any thing to Philpot before these blows, not after he came out of the house—what was said inside I did not notice—I turned round to look at the deceased, to see how he was hurt—the prisoner then caught up the deceased's bundle, and made towards the house—the deceased called out, "Don't rob me as well as murder me"—I followed him into the house with the deceased, and told him the deceased gave him into custody, and as such he must think himself—he then had a conversation with the deceased—he seemed very repentant to him for what had occurred, and wished him not to press any charge against him, as he had nobody to take care of his house, except he called his wife up—the deceased said he would not, and they had some brandy together—the deceased was bleeding at the ear—I took him over to the hospital to have his ear dressed, and left him in the street afterwards—he said he was going home—they did not keep him in the hospital—they sewed the ear up with one stitch—I interfered as soon as I could—they were some distance from me at first.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. They were dreadful blows you got, were they not? A. No, not very—I did not show them to any medical man—when I went into the room, they were chaffing at one another—they were men who had been labouring in the yard, I believe—they all appeared friends—the prisoner wished to clear his house of them, and was persuading them to go—it was a glass of neat brandy they had together after it was over—the prisoner advised me to take the man to the hospital—I called on him next morning, and he went with me to see the man
—they chatted together, and shook hands then—the prisoner appeared very anxious to know how he was—they went back to the public-house together, and drank half-and-half together—I did not hear the prisoner ask him to stay to breakfast, nor hear the deceased say, "I own myself in fault"—they staid together about twenty or twenty-five minutes while I was there—I believe the man lived twelve days after this.
STEPHEN BURTENSHALL . I am a watchman, and live in Little Britain. I was on duty at the Castle and Falcon, between eleven and twelve o'clock on this night—I went into the tap-room, and they were all drinking together in good humour, when I came out—I went in again to call the policeman out, and said the sergeant was going by—the prisoner was then talking along with them—the deceased was laughing and joking with them also—I turned, and came out, and Childs, the deceased, came out laughing directly after-wards—the prisoner followed him with two or three pots in his hand—the deceased came running down the yard, and the prisoner after him twenty-five or thirty yards—he then overtook him, and struck him in the left side of the ear with a quart pot—he then up with his hand to strike him again, and the policeman rushed in between them, but did not reach him to hit him—the policeman asked him something, and he up with both hands to strike the deceased again, but he hit the policeman instead of him, with one hand on the breast, and the other on the top of the shoulder—they stood there talking a little while, and then went in to have something to drink, all of them, but what passed then, I do not know—I saw the deceased at the same public-house next evening, with his head tied up.
Cross-examined. Q. What was he doing there? A. Nothing—I did not see him drinking or smoking—it was about nine o'clock—there were only about five or six people there—I had not seen him at all in the course of the day.
JOHN STEPHENS . I am a surgeon. The deceased was brought to St. Bartholomew's hospital on Wednesday, the 6th of March—he had a wound on the left ear, which divided the ear, and a slight bruise behind the ear—they might have been done by a pot—he was not drunk, he had been drinking—I dressed the ear, and did what I thought necessary—I proposed his stopping in the hospital, but he wished to go home—he seemed a respectable young man—I desired him to come again, but did not see him again alive—I assisted Mr. Lewis in making a post-mortem examination—he died from erysipelas on the head—that might have been occasioned by the blow on the head—he had no signs of erysipelas about him when he came to me.
Cross-examined. Q. Would not drinking raw brandy be very apt to bring it on? A. Not likely to bring it on, but very likely to increase it, it would not cause it without a wound—I should think it a very improper course to pursue after a wound however slight—it did not appear to me a severe wound when he came to me—I put a suture in to unite the two parts, and gave him some opening medicine—he was quite sensible, and did not appear stunned by the blow—if he had taken proper care of himself, and abstained from liquor, and done as I desired him, it is probable erysipelas might not have come on.
DAVID LEWIS . I am a surgeon, and live in Artillery-place, Bunhillrow. I was called to the deceased at twelve o'clock at night, on the 18th of March—I found he had erysipelas on the face and head—that might arise from any cause—it did not appear more than erysipelas in common
—when I was called in I did not inquire about marks on the head only they told me he had acut, for which he had been in the hospital—his face and head were covered with erysipelas—he died on the 27th—I tried the best medicines I could to cure it—they failed—I was present at the opening of the body, and fully agree with Mr. Stephens.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose you also agree with him that drinking ardent spirits, and such things, would be highly improper? A. Certainly—the prisoner behaved in the kindest possible manner during the man's illness—he sent him wine from his house, which was necessary—he told me to do all I could, and he would pay every expense.
MARGARET DENMAN . I attended the deceased during his illness—the erysipelas commenced by the side of his head, near his ear—I had seen him on the 5th, and he seemed quite well then—I believe he was thirty-two years old.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was it before he took to his bed? A. On the 17th—I think he was at home, sitting by the fire-side during the previous week—I am not aware that he went to a public-house—he might have done so without my knowledge—I did not see him on the 7th—I cannot say whether I saw him on the 18th—he was walking about—he frequently called on me in the morning, saying he was going to the hospital—I live next door to him.
MR. PHILLIPS called
THOMAS RAWLINS . I am ostler at the Castle and Falcon. I was in the public-house on the night in question, and saw the deceased there—he said he would have another pot, or else he would have a lark to night—the landlord brought in a pot, and then attempted to shut up the bar—he then went into the room again to gather up the pots—the deceased sat on the table on my right hand, and said he would have a lark —he jumped on the table, put the gas out, and ran away, and the landlord followed him—I saw them together next morning, and heard the deceased say be was more in fault himself than the landlord was the night before.
HENRY COX . I am porter at the Castle and Falcon. On the day after the occurrence, I was in the tap of Philpot's house—the deceased told me he had made it all up, and he was as much to blame as the landlord was—I afterwards went into the tap, and saw him there for three days after-wards, and every time he was taking refreshment, smoking and drinking—the deceased told me the prisoner gave him 5 s. for the loss of his two days' work.
JURY to MR. STEPHENS. Q. Are you able to say whether the erysipelas came on in consequence of the blow, or might it have come on in consequence of drink? A. I think it would not have come on except from the blow, but it became more fatal from drink—I think the blow was quite sufficient cause for erysipelas, but if the man had paid more attention to himself it might not have been fatal.
GUILTY of an Assault only. Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1409]
1409. DAVID JULPH and CHARLES PRICE were indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, at Edmonton, 1 ewe, price 1 l. 5 s., the property of Susannah Nash —2nd COUNT, for killing the same, with intent to steal the carcase; and that they had been previously convicted of felony.
JAMES COCKERELL . I am in the employ of Mrs. Susannah Nash, of Edmonton. I counted her sheep about six o'clock on Tuesday, the 9th
of April, and there were thirty-four ewes—they were in the two-acre field—I counted them again on Wednesday morning, and one ewe was gone, marked with "N"—I found the skin at the top of another field, in the tide of the hedge, the entrails laid by it, and were warm—I have seen two legs, two shoulders, and some more mutton since, and have matched them with the skin—the knuckles came in at the right place—I bare no doubt it belonged to the skin.
GEORGE ANGUS . I work for Mrs. Nash. On Tuesday, the 9th of April, about twelve o'clock, I was in the field minding the sheep, and saw the two prisoners in the road, looking over the hedge into the field where the sheep were—I counted the sheep with Cockerell, in the evening, and there were thirty-four—I missed one next day.
NELSON SMITH . I am a policeman. I was sent for on this occasion—I went into the field and saw footsteps of two persons, which lead to the end of Sweetbriar-walk, about three quarters of a mile from the field—I obtained a search-warrant, and went to Julph's house—he lives with his mother, and Price lodges there occasionally—I found the two prisoners in bed together, at least Julph was in bed, and Price at the foot of the bed, partly dressed—I searched the premises, and found in a box which was locked, two shoulders, two legs, and part of a loin of mutton—I have compared that with the skin, and am certain it belonged to it—I compared the prisoners' shoes with the footmarks in the field, and they quite matched—they are both very peculiar shoes—there was blood on the pair belonging to Price—when I took them, Price attempted to get out of window, but a man I had placed outside prevented him.
Julph. Q. Are you certain the shoes you had belonged to me? A. Yes, they are the shoes you took off—I did not order you to put them on, you had them on—Price had not his shoes on, but he put them on without my asking him to do so.
Julph's Defence. The shoes did not belong to me—I did not steal the sheep.
Prices Defence. I am innocent of it.
JOSEPH ALDER . I produce a certificate of both the prisoners' former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoners are the persons—they were convicted together, and sentenced to be transported for life.
JULPH— GUILTY . Aged 18.
PRICE— GUILTY , Aged 18.
[trialAccount t18390408-1410]
1410. MARY ROWLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of April, 1 quartern of flour, value 9 d. the goods of John Bell.
MARY ANN BELL . I am the wife of John Bell, a corn-chandler. On the 12th of April, about half-past six o'clock, the prisoner came into the shop for a pint of split-peas and some oats—while I was gone round for them, I missed a quartern of flour—I told her she bad got my property in her apron—she said she had nothing of mine—I took hold of her wrist, and took the flour out of her lap—she resisted, and burst the bag open—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. Did she offer to pay for the flour? A. No—she said nothing whatever about it—it was on the counter—she had her apron up before her, and I suspected her.
REBECCA STANNARD . I was in the parlour, and saw the prisoner come
in and ask for a pint of split-peas and a pint of oatmeal—I saw her take the flour off the counter and put it into her apron.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the parlour-door open? A. Yes—Mrs. Bell accused her of it—I went to her assistance, and stopped her—I heard nothing about her paying for it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 49.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18390408-1411]
1411. ELIZABETH ROACH was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of February, 1 breast-pin, value 5 s.; 1 pair of scissors, value 2 s.; 2 spoons, value 3 s.; 2 pillow-cases, value 3 s.; and 2 towels, value 1 s. 10 d.; the goods of Ann Walton, her mistress.
ANN WALTON . I live in Pancras-lane, Queen-street. I employed the prisoner as a charwoman for about twelve months—I lost the articles stated—these are them—( looking at them)—she had the opportunity of taking them—I never authorised her to take them.
JOHN JAMES MOORE . I am a pawnbroker. I have produced all these articles—they were pawned at our house by the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence ( written.)"The prosecutrix took 4 s. 6 d. from my wages for the silk handkerchief—I lived with her twelve months, at 4 s. a week, and went from seven o'clock in the morning till three in the after-noon, frequently without meat or drink—I afterwards returned home to my sick husband without the means of buying a meal."
ANN WALTON re-examined. I had two handkerchiefs lost unconnected with this charge, and I have lost a great many articles, but I have so many persons about me I was afraid to suspect any one in particular—I told her this was the second handkerchief I had lost, and I should take 6 d. A week of her wages for the first—my agreement was that she should come at seven o'clock in the morning and leave at ten, but she thought proper to keep about the house till three—she has no sick husband to my knowledge.
GUILTY . Aged 47.— Confined Three Months.
