1412. JOHN BURNHAM was indicted for an indecent assault.
GUILTY .**— Confined Two Years.
NEW COURT.— Wednesday, April 18 th, 1839.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390408-1413]
1413. EDWARD WILLIAMS and GEORGE DONALD BAYNTON were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN BROWNING . I am shopman to Mr. Folkard, a pawnbroker, in the London-road. On the 17th of December, the prisoner Williams came and asked if I would take in a piece of flannel—I said, "Yes"—he said, "It is the usual quality and quantity that I bring to your house"—he did not bring it then, but he stepped outside, and Baynton came in with him, with this piece of flannel—( producing it)—I asked Williams what he wanted for it—he said 9 l.—I asked what quantity there was—he said 138 or 140 yards—I am confident he said that—Baynton was present, but he took no part—I counted the folds, and found there
was nothing like the quantity he specified—I said, "There cannot be that quantity of yards"—Williams said, "I will forfeit my life if there is not that number"—it was laid in rather better than half-yard lengths—I said I did not consider there was above 90 or a 100 yards—Williams said I was mistaken, they were all marked at the end, and he pointed out to me the mark on the flannel—138 was marked on it in red chalk—I said it was very strange that I could not make that number—he said he would forfeit his existence if there was not that number of yards, which was there marked—he said, "You have taken in flannel of me before, and you never disputed my word before"—he then produced a duplicate from Mr. Jones, a pawnbroker, of a piece of flannel, pawned for 8 l.—he said, "Your neighbours do not doubt my word, and why should you?"—I said, "Well, as you say there is that number of yards I will lend you 6 l., and to end the dispute, I had better measure it"—he said, no, he could not allow that, as he served shops, the flannel was done up on a machine, and if it was undone, neither he nor I could do it up in the same way again—I said then "You had better take what I offer you"—he said that would not do, he must have more money, he said the flannel was worth 3 s. a yard—I said then I would lend him 7 l.—he said, no, I must make it 8 l.—I then said, "Well, I will make it the other half-sovereign"—I lent him 7 l. 10 s., and made out the ticket in the usual way, and gave him the money—I did not put the quantity on the ticket—they then left the shop together—this is the flannel—it has been unpacked, and measured since, and it is 88 yards—I had taken in pieces of flannel of him before, and he has taken them out five or six times.
COURT. Q. Can you form any judgment as to what that is really worth? A. I asked a person in the trade, and he said he could buy it at 1 s. 3 d. a yard.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Would you have advanced the money you did, on that piece of flannel, if you had thought it contained no more than it really does? A. I should not, a shilling a yard is the utmost I should have lent upon it—in the evening of the same day, Baynton came to the shop, and asked me to take in another piece of flannel—he did not bring it—I asked him if it was the same quality—he said, "Yes, just the same"—I asked what it contained—he said the usual quantity, about 138 or 140 yards—he wanted 8 l. on that—I offered him 7 s.—he would not take that—he took 7 l. 10 s.—he went outside, and brought in the flannel—I did not look at the mark on that piece, at the time, but I have measured it since, and there are 80 yards—it appears to me to be the same kind of flannel as the first piece—1 s. a yard is as much as I would have advanced on it—I did not see either of the prisoners again, till I was at the Mansion House—a woman called on me, and from what I heard I measured the flannel.
COURT. Q. In what place of custody had the flannels been kept? A. In our warehouse, I rolled them up myself, and the boy took them up stairs.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How many shopmen have you? A. Two, besides me and the warehouse boy—I had the flannels about a fortnight before I measured them—only those persons who are in the business have access to them—it is not worth more than 1 s. a yard for a pawn-broker to lend.
Q. What is the proportion between what a pawnbroker lends, and the real value? A. Two thirds, if I think a thing would fetch 5 s., I lend 4 s. on it—I am not a judge of flannel—fine flannel would be very cheap it 1 s. 3 d. a yard, but this is very thin—flannels may run from 3 s. to 4 s. or 5 s. a yard—Williams had pawned with me five or six times before, but Baynton had not—Williams has left flannel a week or a fortnight before he redeemed it—on all the other occasions it was flannel he pawned. I had not had disputes with him about the quantity before, as I always made them within ten or a dozen yards of what he stated.
Q. How happened it, as you had all this chaffering, you did not put the number of yards on the duplicate? A. I supposed I was dealing with an honest man—if I had measured it, I should have done it—we are answerable for the number of yards we put on—I believe it is the custom of manufacturers who have articles by them, to pawn them for the purpose of getting ready money, and then redeem them—that is the reason I took the prisoner's word.
COURT. Q. Did you measure either of the pieces that he redeemed? A. No, I did not—he had not left any of them as long as three weeks—it was sometimes only three days.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Hand in the duplicates which relate to these two pawnings? A. Here they are—here is eighty yards on one, and eighty-eight on the other—that was put on about a fortnight after I took the flannel in—at the time I measured this, it was placed in the usual place where we keep pledges—when the discovery took place, they were done up just the same as when I took them in—they appeared to have been done up by a machine—there was no possibility of their having been undone and done up again.
JAMES SMITH . I am shopman to Mr. Summers, a pawnbroker in Bath-street, City-road. On the 4th of January, Baynton came and asked if I would take a piece of flannel in—I said, "How many yards is it?"—he said, "A piece, 138 yards"—I said, "I will look at it"—in a few minute he and Williams came in together, and brought the flannel—I said, "Well, how much?"—Williams said "10 l."—I said "Who is the owner of it?—Williams said, "It is all the same, this is my son," pointing to Baynton—I said, "Well, there is 138 yards, is there?"—Williams said, "No; 140" I said, "One says 138, the other says 140; which am I to understand?" Williams said, "It is a 138 yards piece, but they always run 140 yards"—I said, "How am I to know there is 140 yards?"—he said, "A piece never runs less than 140 yards"—I said, "What, are you the manufacturer?"—he said he was, and had been so for years—I said, "I don't like to take it unless I measure it"—he said, "You must not measure it, you will disturb it"—I said, "You are a manufacturer, are you not? you can do it up if I can't"—he said, "No, it is done with a machine"—I said, "Well, you have machines"—he said, "No, there are none in this town"—I said, "Well, if you will not allow me to undo it, I don't like to take it; I know nothing of you; have you a card?"—he said, "No; but here is the ticket of a pawnbroker, who I have been doing business with a number of years"—he showed it me—I said, "Why don't you take it there again?"—he said, "I don't like to take so much to one place"—I said "This has not been out above a day or two"—he then said, "The carriage conies to so much"—I then began to count the folds, and he stopped me—I said, "We can ascertain a little by that, I think;" and by counting
the folds I made it about 89 yards—he said, "You can't tell any thing by that; I will vouch there is 140 yards, or else I will forfeit my life"—I said I should not like to lend any thing like that upon it—he slid, "What will you lend?"—I said, "Bring me two pieces, and I will lend you 10 l."—he said, "I can't bring two pieces now, what will you lead on this?"—I said, "I will take it in for 1 s. a yard"—he pretended to be astonished at that, saying the value was so much more—he at last left it on what I supposed was 1 s. a yard—he represented it 140 yards, and I lent him 7 l. on it, supposing it was that—I believed it to be 140 yards, oft his representation, he appearing to be a respectable man—Mr. Summers measured it the following day, and thinking he might be mistaken, I measured it myself, four or five days afterwards, and made it the same as he did, 90 yards—here is the counterpart of the duplicate, on which I afterwards put the quantity it measured—this is the duplicate I gave the prisoner—I gave Williams 7 l., deducting the ticket-money—when I gave it him he asked me if would take another piece in in the morning—I said I would ask Mr. Summers, and if he liked to call in the morning, I would give him an answer—I did not see either of the prisoners again till the Monday following, when Baynton brought another piece—I said, "You represented what you brought before as 140 yards, and there is only 90; you know you defrauded me"—he said, "I know there was 140 yards"—I said, "You know there was only 90"—he said, "Then you won't have the other piece?"—I said, "Have the other piece! no, you had better go and mind what you are doing for the future;" and he went away.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long was that after the first pawning? A. The following Monday—I believe the first was on the Friday—I said, "You had better leave me this piece to make up for the one you left," and he made a jeer at me—I said nothing about his paying me the money for the piece he had left, and taking it back again—some articles may be worth nearly the money we lend at the time, but at the end of the year may not be worth half the money—in taking an article like flannel, we estimate the value as if it would remain in out hands for twelve months, and then probably it may not have a good sale—we get fifteen per cent on the money lent—flannel at the end of the year would be much less likely to sell than silver and other things—it is liable to moth and other things—this flannel would fetch more if sold in a shop than at our sale—I think this might fetch 1 s. 6 d. a yard at the sale.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Are you obliged to sell these things by auction at the end of a year? A. Yes—there is the auctioneer's commission to pay, also moving, and other expenses, which we take into account.
JAMES MASTERS HOWIE . I am shopman to Mr. Johnson, of Bishopsgate-street, a pawnbroker. On Saturday, the 5th of January, Baynton asked me if I would take in some flannel—I told him I would, provided it was all right, and I had the owner present when I took it in—he said he would bring it on the Monday, and on Monday both the prisoners came, about three o'clock in the afternoon, with a piece of flannel done up in folds, which I produce—I asked what they wanted upon it, and Williams said, 10 l.—I asked him what quantity of yards it contained—he said, "137," and at the same time he referred me to the mark at the end of the flannel in red chalk, 137—he said that was the mark of the number of yards it contained—I said, "How am I to know that?"—he said, "I am
the manufacturer of the article, and I will forfeit my existence if it is not all right and correct"—I said if that was the case I would advance him money upon it, but I should not like to lend him so much as that—he said he had been obliged to advance money that morning to make up a bill, and showed me a duplicate of a piece he had pledged that morning at Mr. Cotton's for 9 l.—it stated, "Piece of flannel, 137 yards as marked, 9 l."—he solicited me to lend him the same on this piece—I told him I could not, I would lend him 7 l. upon it—I gave him 7 l., and they both left the shop—both the prisoners were present during the conversation—some information afterwards reached me, and on the following Friday, the 11th, Baynton came, and asked if I would take in another piece of flannel—I asked him if it was the same description and quality as the other—he said, "Yes," and he would bring it on the following day—I had a policeman there the next day, and Baynton came about twelve o'clock with a piece of flannel—I asked him the quantity of yards it contained, and if it was the same description as the other—he said, yes, it was of the same description, and much about the same length, 139 yards, and I found a mark of 139 on the end—I said if there was the quantity of yards I would advance him the same money as I had done on the piece on Monday—I took a duplicate out of the drawer, and pretended to write it out for him—the officer then came into the shop, and I said, "I shall not take this piece in without measuring it"—Baynton said, "You must not do that, you will spoil the appearance of the flannel," and he referred me to the mark on it—I said I felt confident that was not the case, that I had been defrauded in one instance by himself and the elder party who was with him, and he was now attempting it again—he said the property did not belong to him, but to his father, who was waiting outside—while this was going on Williams came in, and asked Baynton what be was doing there so long, or why he was waiting—Baynton replied that he was given into custody—Williams asked who by, and if there was any money given for the piece—I said, no, and as I suspected this piece was the same as the last, I should give the officer charge of him also—I gave charge of them both—these are the pieces of flannel—the first piece taken on the 7th of January has been measured, and contains 79 yards—both ends of this are marked—on the outer end is 137, and on the inner end 78—on the one brought on the 11th is 139—this has been measured, and contains 86 yards.
COURT. Q. Are you acquainted with the trade so as to know what the outside mark denotes? A. No; it was represented to me as the quantity it contained.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. You have put down the number I see on the duplicate? A. Yes, I did that when I got it.
HENRY RICHARDSON . I am shopman to Mr. Cotton, a pawnbroker, in Shoreditch. In the beginning of January Williams came to my master's shop—he asked if I would take in some pieces of flannel to the amount of 10 l. or 20 l.—I said I would not take in more than 10 l.—he said he was a manufacturer of it, and it was a bad time of year to dispose of it—on the 7th of January he called with two pieces of flannel, and Baynton was then with him—it must have been between twelve and one o'clock—the two pieces were put on the counter, and Williams represented that there were 37 yards in each piece, referring me to a mark at the end of them in red chalk—I thought, from the previous conversation I had had, that that was the 10 l. worth, and I was going to make a ticket for 10 l. with very little
conversation on the subject—Williams then said he wanted 10 l. on each piece, and that I had misunderstood him—I said I could not think of lending 10 l. on each piece, that I did not consider they were of that value to me and I would not take in more than one piece—he said, "137 yards of fine flannel surely you will lend me 10 l. on?"—I said I could not—I reckoned it up, and offered him 9 l. on it—he agreed to take it—I was going to make out the ticket, and I said, "It is rather awkward, how am I to call this a piece of flannel?" and he showed me a ticket of Mr. Fleming's, on which was "137 yards of fine flannel, as marked," and I made mine out the same—he referred me to the mark of 137 on the flannel—I made out the ticket on the faith of what he represented, and they went away—on the same day I received a communication from Mr. Cotton, and just after that Baynton came to the shop again—I was in the parlour—I asked him into the parlour—I said I was very much afraid I could not make the number of yards, I had tried it, and could not make more than 100—he said he was certain there was, and referred me to the mark—he said it was measured in the manufacturing districts—I asked if he would allow me to measure it—he said no, it could not be done up in London in the same form, and it would spoil the sale—I said it placed me in a very awkward situation—he said he would go and speak to his father.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Did Williams, when you took the first piece, say there was 137 yards? A. He both pointed to the mark and said there was—I have taken in small quantities of flannel at different times—I suppose this to be worth 1 s. 6 d. or 1 s. 10 d. a yard—I consider at our sale it would fetch that—it is a bad time to sell flannel in January, February, or March, and that I took into consideration, as we should have to sell it at a lower rate.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you measured it since? A. Yes, there are 94 yards—there are marks at each end—on the inner end is "1734," and on the top "137."
FRANCIS COTTON . I am a pawnbroker. I have one shop in Shoreditch, and one in Hackney-road—on the 7th of January I was in my shop in Hackney-road—about the middle of the day the two prisoners came in and brought a piece of flannel, but I did not take it in—it was in flat folds—I forget the sum they asked for it, but it was 9 l. or 10 l.—I asked what quantity there was, and they said the quantity was marked on it, 137, pointing to a red chalk mark on the outer end—my young man was attending to the prisoners, and I asked him if he had measured it—he said, "No"—I said I could not tell what there was without—they said they would not have it measured on any account, as it would put it out of condition, and those marks on the flannel were made in the country by authorised registered men—they showed me a ticket which my young man had given them, and that of Mr. Fleming's.
Williams's Defence. They were my own goods, and I meant no harm—my step-son merely carried a piece or two—he knows nothing about it. (The prisoners received good characters.)
WILLIAMS— GUILTY — Confined Three Months.
BAYNTON— GUILTY — Confined One Month.

1416. JOHN THOMPSON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
SUSANNAH BURNS . I am the wife of Patrick Burns, he belongs to the Royal Artillery. On the 9th of March we were at the Tower—the prisoner who was a stranger, came to me and inquired for my husband, Patrick Burns—he said he was my mother's brother's son—he said he had come from Ireland, and he had brought me a box weighing four cwt. containing tea and sugar, soap, chocolate, seven silk handkerchiefs, and some silk dresses, a cage and two parrots—he said he had brought it from abroad for me, and asked if I had any money, he wanted 2 s. 6 d. he said, to release this box from the Custom-house—I gave him half-a-crown—he said that was not sufficient, and I gave him a shilling more—he said, "Cousin, don't think I want to do you any harm, don't think I would take any thing from you, I would rather give you something"—he sat down a few minutes, and then went away, and I never saw him again till my husband took him—I never got my box—my husband met him on the Sunday, the next day, and said, "Come along with me, you have robbed my wife"—he said, "Take my coat and hat, and 11 d. in halfpence, and let me go"—he said, "No, I will give you in charge."
THOMAS COOPER . I am an officer. I took the prisoner into custody from Patrick Burns.
Prisoner's Defence. I did it from perfect hunger and distress—I had not had a morsel of food for two days—I had been for two nights on the bridges of London.
GUILTY .* Aged 31.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18390408-1417]
1417. WILLIAM KEENE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
CHRISTOPHER GARNER . I am a labouring man residing in Kent. I became entitled to some premises at Shore ham, and at Horsham, which was left me by my grandfather, and considering the prisoner an attorney, I employed him to get possession for me—I employed him as a lawyer, and had paid him before this from 15 l. to 20 l.—when I spoke to the prisoner about it, he told me he was clerk to Mr. Collyer, in Chancery-lane, and that be was authorised to act for him, and to use Mr. Collyer's name—he said he was carrying on the proceedings to aid me in getting possession of my property—he told me he wanted money to carry that on—I gave him 5 l. on the 9th of March, in consequence of what he said—he was to carry on the business for me—I was to go to Maidstone, and see him there on the 11th of March, but he was not there—I never got my 5 l. back.
Prisoner. Q. Have you got any account of the money you paid me? A. No, I know I paid you from 15 l. to 20 l.—I did not tell you when you wrote to me for money in the country to write for twice as much as you wanted, to deceive my sister—you wrote to me once for 1 l., and when I came you wanted two.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not accompany you to Kemsden, and to Shoreham, and serve ejectments? A. You served something, I do not know what—my sister saw all the letters that came, for I could not read them.
SAMUEL COLLYER . I am a solicitor, and live in Chancery-lane. I
have known the prisoner twenty-four or twenty-five years in different offices, but neither he nor any other person was authorised to use my name in law proceedings—I knew nothing of this transaction till the prosecutor called on me.
Primer. Q. Have you never authorised me to use your name? A. Never, I never gave you authority to lodge detainers against persons in custody—I signed two discharges for persons in Whitecross-street prison, Prisoner. You know there is an application about being made to the Court this term, on account of your allowing unqualified men to use your name. Witness. I know nothing about it. I know a person of the name of Wood, he is my clerk—over my door is E. A. Wood, and Mr. Collyer's offices—he is the housekeeper—both the houses belong to him—I have no person whom I allow to practice in my name—I was in the habit of calling on you almost every Sunday when you lived in Clerkenwell, but I never settled an account with you—I have a writing clerk of the name of Lewis Dolby—he is paid according to his labour—I never asked you to accept a bill for 200 l.—I never heard of such a thing—I never asked you how you came on when you went into Kent—I never knew you went there—I had two or three letters from Kent—I do not know who they came from—I received a packet containing 4 l. 15 s., and I gave it to you.
JOHN VERYAN . I am a porter in Farringdon-market. I know the prosecutor—on Saturday the 9th of March, I took 5 l. to the prisoner, on account of the prosecutor—I put it down, and he told me to take half-a-crown for my trouble—here is the bill he gave we for it.
Prisoner. Q. Ought not that to have come on the Friday night? A. Yes, but it did not come till Saturday.
( The prisoner read a long defence, stating that the prosecutor had employed him in the business, and that he communicated his proceedings to Mr. Collyer, who was satisfied—that he had no intention to defraud the prosecutor; and that Mr. Collyer had authorised him to use his name.)
GUILTY. Aged 50. —Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1418]
1418. WILLIAM HAWKES was indicted for a misdemeanor.
SETH BULL . I am a victualler. I had bottled beer of Mr. Drybrough—he used to allow me 3 s. a dozen for the empty bottles—on the 2nd of March the prisoner came to me for Mr. Drybrough's bottles, and I delivered them to him—he said he came from Mr. Drybrough for the empty bottles—I cannot say exactly that I believed him, because I had had a note from Mr. Drybrough a fortnight before, saying he had been robbed to a great extent, and he would send his book with the person who came for them in future—I let the prisoner have six dozen of bottles, and then I asked to look at his book, and he had not got one—he took the bottles off my premises—I followed him and came up with him—he made some excuse, stating that the man would bring the book presently—I took him into custody—the man did not come with the book.
Prisoner. Q. How far did I take them? A. Not more than two or three yards—you carried them off my premises—I detained you, and told you to bring them in, which you did—you called on me two days before, and I should have given you them then, but I had not time.
ANDREW DRYBROUGH . I serve the prosecutor with bottled beer—I did not send the prisoner for any bottles—I never saw him in my life till at Worship-street—he went quite without my knowledge.
Prisoner's Defence. I was engaged by a wine-merchant's porter with a truck—he gave me 1 s. 6 d. to help him draw it—he stopped near Mr. Bull's house—took a basket off and said, "Go to that house, as I come back I will call for you and take the bottles"—I went, and he gave me six dozen and four bottles—I rolled them to the door-post—I could not carry them—Mr. Bull then gave me into custody—he acknowledged at the office that his wife told him that two men came with a truck while he was giving me into custody.
GUILTY . * Aged 45.— Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18390408-1423]
1423. JAMES CHARLES CLIFFORD was indicted for wilful and corrupt perjury.
MESSRS. JAMES and GIBBS conducted the Prosecution.
— SCARLETT. I am marshal to Lord Abinger. I produce the record on which an action was brought on a bill of exchange—this is the bill which was produced at the trial—I have had it ever since.
GEORGE FREDERICK HUDSON . I am a solicitor, and live in Bucklers-bury. I was concerned for the present defendant in 1835—he sent for me to the Queen's Bench Prison, where he was in custody, and gave me a memorandum of his demand against Lady Parker—the paper I have in my hand, I believe are the written instructions he gave me—they are his handwriting—( reads)—"Lady Fanny Parker to J. C. Clifford.—1836: March 11, Bill 40 l.; June 30, ditto 21 l. 15 s. 8 d.; up to July 13, ditto 1 l. 6 s.: Total 63 l. 1 s. 8 d."—I lodged a detainer against Lady Parker—60 l. I think was the amount sworn to—I proceeded in the action—these are the particulars of it—( looking at some papers)—the cause came on for trial, and the cause and all matters and differences were referred.
Q. Is this the order of reference? ( handing a paper to the witness) A. This is a copy, I rather think—I am not certain it is a copy—I believe it is the original.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. On your oath, are you prepared to swear positively that it is the original? A. No, I would not—I believe that it is.
Q. Look at this paper; read that on the back ?—(reads)—A. "Clifford versus parker. Copy Order of Reference."
MR. JAMES. Q. What is the ordinary practice; are there two originals made out on paper, one for each party? A. I believe there are.
COURT. Q. When a case is referred at Nisi Prius, there are two parties, the plaintiff and defendant, and do you not know that there are two papers given? A. I believe there are.
( Read)—"12th December, in the seventh year, &c. This cause, and all matters and differences, including the rule now pending, between the two parties, are hereby referred to John Busby, Esq., Barrister-at-law, to order what he shall think proper to be done."
Witness. I or my clerk took up the original award, but I have not got it, as I have since delivered all my papers to Mr. Clifford's then attorney.
Q. In February, 1837, was any money paid to you by Mr. Austin in respect of that action? A. There was—this is the receipt I gave him for 128 l. 10 s., in full, for the debt and costs—the debt was 40 l. and the rest were the costs—I never saw Mr. Clifford after I gave that receipt—I sent him the balance due to him, which was 26 l.—there were 14 l., the costs of a rule due from Clifford to me, and I think these were costs out of pocket—I deducted all that was due to me, and sent him the balance—I never saw him again on the subject of tins action.
Q. From the time the action commenced, till the settlement, was any thing ever said to you by Clifford about two bills? A. I believe there never was—he said nothing to me at that time, except about one bill—( looking at a bill)—I believe this to be the bill on which I brought the action.
COURT. Q. What do you mean by the word "believer A. It is now three or four years ago, one does not like to speak positively.
MR. JAMES. Q. Have you a moral certainty in your own mind that that is the same bill? A. I have; I must have had it at the time I took the instructions, or shortly after—my attention was not called particularly to it more than to any other transaction—the prisoner was in the habit of tending from the Bench to me—I have, received letters from Mm, in his handwriting, by French—I sent the money to the prisoner, I think, by my clerk, in February—and in March, 1837, I received a communication from him, and I sent him, by French, the bill on which the action was brought, and some cheques—I believe I sent the bill on which the action was brought—I had no other, but I have said that I am not certain that this is the bill.
Q. Is it your belief, to the best of your memory, that yon sent back this bill of exchange with the cheques by French? A. I cannot speak any other way than to the best of my recollection—not having seen the document for three years, I will not speak to it more positively—I believe that bill had no indorsement beside Clifford's—that bill was in the same state as this, except that it had not Gibbs's indorsement, which it now has—to the best of my recollection, this is the bill certainly.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Have you not sworn you were not certain that it was the bill? A. I am not certain other than to the best
of my belief—I will not speak to the certainty of a document I have not seen for three years.
Q. You said you had a moral certainty, what did you mean by that? A. I will give you my definition of it—that while there is a possibility of its not being so from some exact imitation of it, still, as there is no ground for believing it has been imitated, I believe it to be the same—when we see a bill we sometimes see it only once—I saw that perhaps half-a-dozen times—I believe there was no other indorsement on it—I will not say my recollection of it must have been very slight—the reason I will not swear positively to it is because I have not seen it for three years.
COURT. Q. The bill was produced before the arbiter? A. Yes, I had the bill there—the signature to these two affidavits are the prisoner's writing, and this paper of instructions is his writing, ( read.)
In the first affidavit, dated 13th September, 1838, the defendant deposed that Lady Parker was not detained in the King's Bench on or about the 14th July, 1836, on the 40 l. bill on which the present action was brought, but on another bill of the same date and amount, which the said Lady Parker had accepted in addition to the first—in the second affidavit, dated 20th October, 1838, he deposed, that when he first instructed his attorney, Mr. Hudson, in 1836, he delivered to him a bill of exchange for £40 on Lady Parker, and that he never bad the said bill back from Mr. Hudson, nor had he, to the best of his recollection, ever seen the same since.
LADY FANNY PARKER . I am widow of the late Sir Hyde Parker—I reside in Park-lane—in the year 1835 I employed the defendant as cook—I was then in the King's Bench—I was indebted to the defendant for cooking—he applied to me for money about December, 1835, and I gave him a bill on the 16th of December—I think Mrs. Grimes was present when the defendant made his application for money—I said I expected money from a verdict against Lord Lake, and that I could not give him any money then—40 l. was the amount I then owed him—I think, to the best of my recollection, he delivered me an account at the time he made the application for some money—I then offered to give him a bill, and a bill was brought up to me to accept, which I did accept, in presence of Mrs. Grimes—the amount was 40 l.—this is the bill I accepted at that time—I did not accept two bills, only this one.
COURT. Q. Did you at any time accept two bills of the same date, and for the same amount, 40 l.? A. No, that I am sure of—I swear the prisoner made no application to me for payment of a second bill of 40 l.
MR. GIBBS. Q. Did Clifford, after that, bring an action against you for that bill? A. Yes, sir, in 1836, I think—the amount of that bill, the debt and costs in that action were paid by Mr. Austin, my attorney—this is an affidavit I made in the cause—the signature is my hand-writing, and the contents of it are true—( read)—This affidavit was dated the 6th of September, 1838, in which the deponent swears, that on or about the 14th of July, 1836, she was detained, in the Bench at the suit of Clifford, and that the particulars of the plaintiff's demand, 88 l. 10 s., and bill 40 l. was, about the 20th of February, paid to the plaintiff's attorney; and that she never, to the best of her recollection, had any other transactions or accounts with Clifford; and that she never, to the best of her recollection or belief, accepted any other bill for 40 l. but the one mentioned in the declaration."
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. It was in 1835 you employed the
prisoner as your cook? A. Yes, I went there in 1833, But it was not immediately after that I employed him—I did not give him the money when be first applied—I gave him one bill for 40 l., that was the whole of his claim on me at that time—I owed him no more money whatever—he did not give me a bill at that time, to my recollection—I am sure no account was delivered to me at that time—I gave the bill on the 16th of December, in the large room in the state-house—Mrs. Grimes was present—the prisoner had had a cheque or two of mine, two, I think, they were duly paid—every thing was paid at that time but that 40i.—I had had frequent negotiation with him before this bill—I paid him generally in cash in small sums—I never accepted a bill before—I hive never given him a cheque since that time, and all the cheques I gave him prior to that time have been duly paid to the prisoner—I know this cheque for 9 l. 4 s., 7 d., this was included in the money Mr. Austin paid—it was all paid at that time—I am sure this was included—it is prior to December, 1835—when I gave the bill Mrs. Grimes was present—I don't know whether she read the bill—she was close to me, it was at breakfast time, about ten or half-past ten o'clock—I am quite positive there was no other bill that I signed—I never gave any cheque as late at 1836.
MR. GIBBS. Q. Was this one of the cheques you gave previous to the 40 l. bill? A. Yes, it is in October, 1835—it was paid at the time of the reference to Mr. Busby.
COURT. Q. Has Mr. Austin indemnified you for the expense of this? A. No.
DANIEL EDWIN AUSTIN . I paid the sum of 128 l. 10 s. to Mr. Hudson—I had not a bill of exchange in the action Clifford versus Parker returned to me by Mr. Hudson, nor did I ask for the award—I have never had the bill from Clifford—I applied to him and Mr. Hudson for it.
COURT. Q. You never received the bill of exchange on which this action was brought, and which was the subject of the case referred? A. No, the order of Nisi Prius was for all matters in difference—there was nothing said about another bill of exchange for 40 l., if there had it would have been embraced in the order.
GEOROIANA BEATRICE GRIMES . I am a widow. I reside at Blackheath—I resided with Lady Parker while she was in the Bench, merely as her friend—I don't remember from what time, I think it was about 1833 or 1834, I was there at the latter end of 1835—I remember the defendant calling on Lady Parker for the payment of his bill—I was present—I don't remember that I saw the amount of his claim—her ladyship gave him a bill of exchange to the amount of 40 l.—I saw Lady Parker write upon the bill—she did not give more than one bill—after that bill became due I saw the defendant upon it—he did not state to me then that he had two bills on Lady Parker to the same amount.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. When the prisoner came to Lady Parker for this bill, did he state what the amount of his claim was? A. I don't recollect that he did—he asked for the amount of his bill, but dm not state the amount that I recollect—he did not talk long about the bill, I don't think he was there ten minutes—he asked her if she could pay him his bill—she said she could not, because she had an action pending against Lord Lake, and till that was settled she could not pay him—he then asked her to give him a bill—she said yes—he went out and returned with the bill, which I saw Lady Parker sign—I did not read the bill, but I
heard him say it was for 40 l.—he did not say there was more due—Lady Parker never gave any other bill that I know of, neither before nor after—she has frequently given cheques—I don't know how she generally paid the prisoner.
THOMAS PROTHEROE . I am a clerk in the Exchequer Writ Office—I produce an affidavit of debt in the cause of John Rafter against Fanny Parker—the writ is annexed to it.
JOHN THOMPSON . I was articled to Mr. Kirk, Mr. Clifford's late attorney. He was solicitor in the cause of Rafter against Parker—the signature to this affidavit is the signature of Rafter—I think both the prisoner and Rafter came to the office to give instructions to pursue the action—the prisoner was repeatedly there—I cannot say whether he advanced any money to Mr. Kirk, I believe not.
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. Was Mr. Kirk concerned in either of these actions? A. Yes, he took Mr. Rafter's retainer—this bill was often spoken of—I saw Mr. Hudson repeatedly—in preparing the brief it was necessary to have all the papers—he stated he had a faint recollection of two bills, but he had seen only one, he thought the other one was about 10 l.—it was a small amount—I think two or three cheques had been given to the prisoner, by Lady Parker, after the award.
JAMES BUCKINGHAM . I am an engineer, and live in Camden-town. I am a client of Mr. Austin's, and know him—in consequence of what he wished me to do, on the 17th of January last, I saw the prisoner, I think it was in Braddam's-row, Newington Causeway, I believe it was his house or lodgings—I had received instruction that he was to be found there—I had never seen him before—he asked me if I was aware for what purpose I had come—(he expected me to come to be a witness on the trial, as Rafter's action was then going on)—Mr. Thompson took me there, and introduced me to the prisoner as Mr. Buckingham—the prisoner asked Thompson if I was the person whom he (Thompson) had mentioned to him—Thompson said, "Yes," and that I was a friend of his, and I had come relative to the matter of which they had been speaking—I asked the prisoner what it was he wanted me to do—he said, "I want you to swear that you went with Thompson to Pitt-street"—(it afterwards transpired that Rafter lived there)—he said I must swear that I went with Thompson, and presented the bill at Drummond's, at Charing-cross—that I was to swear that the bill was not paid—that I then accompanied Thompson to lady Parker's house, which he afterwards told me, was No. 13, Park-lane—he told me that was all he wished me to swear—he then addressed Thompson, and told him that he must not only swear what I have mentioned he told me, but that he went afterwards to the King's Bench by himself, bringing the bill, which had been dishonoured, back to Lady Parker, and that it was presented at Lady Parker's, in Park-lane, for payment—after seeing the prisoner in Braddam's-row, I saw him at a coffee-shop, in Newcastle-street, Strand, on the 28th of January—I had appointed to meet him there, through Rafter, whom I had met the previous morning—I was at the coffee-house before the prisoner was—he came in and wished me to accompany him to Mr. Kirk, who, I believe, was his attorney, and was bringing that action—I was to go to have the evidence taken, which he had detailed to me, and he gave me this piece of paper as instructions to swear—he brought the paper written, and I indorsed it within a few minutes—a person of the name of Jenkins was at the coffee-shop, for the purpose of detecting this scheme—I
read this paper over several times, and asked the prisoner particularly if it was his writing—he acknowledged it to be his writing, and said that was what he wished me to swear—he had previously said he would give me 10 s. if I would swear, in the Court of Exchequer what he had stated, and when he gave me this paper he said he would give me 14 s.
(The instructions were as follows:—" Went with Mr. Thompson to Drummond's the bankers, at Charing-cross, to present bill of exchange, 40 l., three months, drawn on Parker, paid to Mr. Clifford, endorsed over to Gibbs, and paid to Rafter—have been at Rafter's house in Pitt-street, have seen Gibbs there several times, and know his hand-writing.")
Cross-examined by MR. LORD. Q. By whose request did you go to see the prisoner? A. Mr. Austin's—he stated what he wanted me to go for—I had known Austin some time, but never saw the prisoner till the 17th of January—I went to the house with Thompson, and asked to see Mr. Clifford—Thompson told me it was Mr. Clifford, and he assented to it—I neverperformed such a duty fox Austin before, nor any one else—I asked the prisoner if I was to swear what he required, what was I to have—he looked at Thompson, and said, "I suppose we must give him the same at you have," and then Clifford said he would give me 10 s.—I said it was very little, and asked him when he should require me—he said the trial would come on on Monday, and he should require me to be at the Exchequer, in the City—I said, "I suppose Mr. Rafter will pay me"—Rafter was. sitting there at the time—I asked who I was sitting in company with, and the prisoner said, "That is Mr. Rafter"—I said, "Let me look at you, stand up, in case I should be asked, I shall be able to recognise you"—I said, "What is your height?"—he said, "Five feet six inches and three quarters"—no money was paid me—I did not agree to perform this office, I said, "If I did it"—I never went by any other name than Buckingham—I have been an engineer four or five years, under my own patent—I have lived in Camden-town since 1834—I was an agent before I was an engineer—I was never charged with any offence in a court of justice.
EDWARD JENKINS . I am an appraiser and broker, and live in Southampton-street. I do the business of Covent-garden parish—I went, at the request of Mr. Austin, to a coffee-shop in Newcastle-street, but I cannot say when—I went by myself, and met Mr. Buckingham there—after he had been there some time, the defendant came in, and sat in the next box—the purport of my being there was to overbear what passed-a conversation took place between Clifford and Buckingham respecting a bill of exchange, which Buckingham was to swear he had protested, and Buckingham wanted to know what he was to be paid—Clifford said 10 s.—he said that would not pay him, he must have more, and Clifford agreed to give him 14 s.—he gave him a paper of instructions, on which was written what he was to do—this is the paper—Buckingham read it over and said, "This, I understand, is what I am to do"—Clifford said, "Yes"—Buckingham then went out, and I went immediately after—I was in Court when the cause came on to be tried, and I heard the defendant deny that this paper was his writing most distinctly, or that he had given it to any one.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you in the same room? A. Yes, there were only us three—it is a small room—I did not speak at all—I was there half an hour—it was about eleven or twelve o'clock—Thompson was not there I am certain.
For the defendant, MR. LORD called JACOB CASTELL. I know the prisoner—I have seen him write, but I do
not know that I should be able to swear to his handwriting exactly-( looking at the instructions)—this paper looks like his writing—I have been employed by him to make out accounts occasionally—I have drawn several bills for him—I drew two bills between him and Lady Parker, on the 16th of December, 1835—I only drew the body of the bills—they were for 40 l. each—I put in the dates—the reason I drew them for Clifford was because his hand shook so—this is one of the bills—( looking at it)—I did not see the bill after it was drawn, till it was shown to me before I was subpoenaed on the trial of Rafter against Parker—I was subpoenaed on the part of Rafter, and gave evidence—I saw Mr. Buckingham, and had some conversation with him on the Monday evening, four days before that trial—a person of the name of John Thompson came to my room, and he took me to the Brace, and introduced me to Buckingham—Thompson said, "This is Mr. Buckingham; he is going to corroborate my evidence on Thursday, and we are all to be of one mind"—I said I did not know what they were going to prove; all I knew was, I was the drawer of the bill that had been shown me, and I was going to prove it—I said I drew two bills, but whether they were accepted or not I did not know.
MR. JAMES. Q. You drew two bills, did you? A. Yes—the whole of this bill is my hand-writing, except the signature of the drawer—I wrote the direction—Clifford did not sign it in my presence—I did not see it after it was drawn—one of the bills I drew that any was on a wrong stamp, a stamp that would not carry the amount.
GUILTY .— Confined One Month in Newgate, and Transported for Seven Years.
ESSEX CASE.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390408-1424]
1424. JOHN HANCOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of March, 2 pairs of boots, value 5 s. 6 d., the goods of John Honey.
JOHN HONEY . I live at Stratford, in Essex, and am a shoemaker. On the 28th of March, about a quarter to nine o'clock in the evening, I was in the room adjoining my shop—I saw the prisoner in the shop, taking two pairs of boots out of the window—he must have lifted the latch to come in—I saw him leave the house, and put the boots in the corner of the next house, which is a public-house, and then cross the road—I followed and secured him—I left him in care of the neighbours, and went and got the boots—I took him about twenty yards from the shop—he saw me following him, and ran.
JOHN BARTLETT . I am a constable of West Ham. The prisoner was given into my custody, with the boots.
Prisoner's Defence. I was coming from llford, on the other side of the road—two boys ran by me, nearly knocked me down with the boots, and chucked them inside the public-house.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
KENT CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390408-1425]
1425. MARY ANN POTTS and MARY ANN WILLIS were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering, the dwelling-house of Thomas Davis, on the 31st of March, at Woolwich, and stealing therein 1 hat, value 2 s., the goods of George Thomas; 1 looking-glass and frame, value 9 s.; and 1 blanket, value 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Davis; and that Potts had been previously convicted of felony.
GEORGE THOMAS . I am a pensioner and lodge in the kitchen of Thomas Davis, in Powis-street, Woolwich. On Sunday, the 31st of March, between seven and eight o'clock, I missed the articles stated from the front kitchen—they had been all let to me with the lodging, except the hat, which was my own—I had seen them safe about six o'clock, when I went up stairs with the landlord—the door leading to the area was then shut, not locked, bat on the latch—when I came down between seven and eight o'clock, it was open, and the area gate also, and the things gone—there are a few steps down to the area.
Potts. Q. You say your door was latched, it was open? A. No, it was latched—I have not found the pillow, only the glass and hat—I did not see you take them.
JOSEPH BUTTERFILL . I am a constable of Woolwich. I produce a blanket which I got from Mrs. Wood, on Sunday night, the 31st of March, also a glass, which I got from a person named Cross, at the house where the prosecutor lodges, and a hat which I received from Sarah Miles, with some baskets—I went in search of the prisoners, and found them at the Coach and Horses public-house, High-street, Woolwich—I told them I took them in charge for robbing Mr. Thomas of a blanket, a glass, and a hat—Potts denied all knowledge of it—I told her I had got a blanket from Mrs. Wood, which had been left by her the same evening—she denied it—Willis also denied the charge, and said she had received the glass from Potts.
Potts. When you took me you did not name-any thing but the blanket. Witness. Yes, I did, all that was told me, a feather pillow, a hat, and a looking-glass, and you denied knowing any thing about them.
WILLIAM CROSS . I am a private in the Royal Marines at Woolwich. On Sunday, the 31st of March, the two prisoners came to my house, on Church-hill, between nine and ten o'clock in the evening—Willis said she wished to leave the glass till to-morrow morning—that they wanted to dispose of it for 4 s., as they were in distress, and it belonged to Potts—my wife said she could not buy it—she said, if my wife could lend her 1 s., as she was in distress, and had had no bread all day, she should be obliged.—I lent her 8 d., and they went away—I afterwards went in search of the prisoners, and found them at the Coach and Horses—I desired Willis to, come and fetch the glass away, as my wife was very uneasy that it was left—she was just in the act of coming with me, when the constable came in and took her—I asked her if it was come by honestly—she said it belonged to Potts, and Potts said it bad belonged to a seaman—I took the glass to Mr. Davis, and while I was there, the officer came and took it.
Potts. You said if it was stolen, you would put it away? Witness. I said so, and if you had said it was stolen, I should have given you into custody—Willis said it belonged to you.
SARAH MILES . I live at Woolwich. On Sunday night, the 31st of March, I was at the Coach and Horses—the two prisoners came in with two friends, and a sailor came in after, but who he was, or whether he was in their company, I cannot say—Potts had some baskets which she left on
the settle—the landlord told me to take them to Butterfill at the cage, which I did—the hat was in them.
MARGARET WOOD . I am the wife of Alexander Wood, and live at Hop-yard Rails, Woolwich. Between eight and nine o'clock, on Sunday evening, the 31st of March, Potts came to me and showed me a blanket which she wanted to sell—she said it belonged to a young man a sailor, and asked me to let her leave it till the morning—I did so—she looked very ill, and asked me to give her 2 d. to get a pint of beer—I gave her 3 d.—she said she would fetch the blanket away in the morning—I gave it up to Butterfill and the prosecutor, who is Potts's uncle—Potts had been to me with Willis that evening with a looking-glass which they wanted to part with, but I refused buying it—I understood Potts to say it belonged to the sailor.
Potts. Q. Did you ever know me do any wrong to any one? A. No, I never knew any thing amiss of you.
GEORGE THOMAS re-examined, I am Potts's uncle. She was not in the habit of coming to my premises—she has been two or three times, but I never asked her to sit down—these are my things ( looking at them)—the pillow has not been found.
Potts's Defence. I have been for twelve months in a Union poor-house at Cambridge—I left about five weeks ago, and came to Willis, having no home, money or clothes—she offered me shelter, gave me part of what she had, and gave me money to procure cotton for my work—I showed a person my uncle's house, but did not take the property; all I took away was a feather pillow—I hope you will be lenient to me—I am sorry for what I have done—this woman is a widow, and has two children—her husband has been dead twelve months—she works from eight in the morning till six in the evening—had we not been under the influence of liquor we should not have done it.
WILLIAM THOMAS CHITTENDEN . I am a constable—I produce a certificate of Potts's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Straights office ( read)- I was present at her trial at Maid stone in 1833—she is the same person.
Potts. Q. How do you know I am the same person? A. I was present and saw you tried—you were committed from Lewis ham.
Potts. You thought this would give me a little help, did you? what business had you to enter on that charge?—I suffered the law of my country for it—I robbed a good master and mistress—I hope, my Lord, my former commitment will not be against me, and that you will be as lenient as you can—I am sorry for what I have done.
POTTS — GUILTY. * Aged 28, of stealing only. — Transported for Seven Years.
WILLIS— GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Six Months.

1427. JAMES JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of Owen Rowland, from his person.
OWEN ROWLAND . I am a porter. On the 2nd of April I was in Greenwich fair—I felt a tug at my right-hand pocket, and missed my handkerchief from it—I saw the prisoner walking away from me, and looking confused—I took hold of him, and said, "I believe you have my handkerchief"—he said he had not—I began to search him—he would not allow me to do so—the policeman came up and found the handkerchief between his legs in his trowsers.
JAMES WILD . I am an officer. I saw the prisoner and prosecutor straggling together—I took the handkerchief out of the prisoner's trowsers—he had no pocket.
Prisoner's Defence. I picked it off the ground—I did not take it from the gentleman.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1428]
1428. EDWARD POTTER was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN KING . I am butler to Mr. Joseph Sladden, of Lea, in Kent. I was in the habit of sending parcels by Mrs. Walker's errand-cart—the prisoner was the servant employed—on the evening of the 26th of February, 1838, or the following morning, I paid him 3 s. 6 d. for parcels on his mistress's account.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you quite sure you paid it all? A. Yes, quite; I had a memorandum of it at the time—I cannot find it—it was the last sum I paid him.
Mr. CLARKSON. Q. What was the parcel? A. A portmanteau, and bag of my young master's.
FANNY WALKER . I am a widow, and carry on the business of a carrier at Eltham. In February last year the prisoner was in my service, and drove my errand-cart—He received money for parcels—I keep a book in which I enter the money received—I have it here—he should account to me for what he received on the following morning—he did not account to me for 3 s. 6 d. at any time from Mr. Sladden—I discharged him on the 9th of November—he gave me no account of its being unpaid—after he left my service I sent in a bill to Mr. Sladden, and found it had been paid.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was the prisoner in your service? A. Five years—he has been in the service of another carrier since I discharged him, but I have not seen him—he has been in custody about a month—I wanted to see what amount he had robbed me of before I prosecuted—this is my book—( producing it)—the entries are in my own hand-writing, and were made at the time—I never made any mistake, to my knowledge, in the accounts.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You sent in a bill of 25 s. to Mr. Sladden? A. Yes—I saw King, the butler—he could only recollect paying this 3 s. 6 d., but he had received altogether from him 10 s. 6 d.
JOHN KING re-examined. The prosecutrix sent me a bill for 25 s.—I had paid 10 s. 6 d. of it altogether—I recollected the 3 s. 6 d. directly, but I could not give a date to the other payments.
JAMES ADAMS . I am the superintendent of the Lewisham watch. I took the prisoner into custody on the 9th of March, and told him I wanted him for some money relating to Mrs. Walker's accounts—he said he knew very well what it was—that he owed her about 30 s.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390408-1429]
1429. THOMAS NICHOLSON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of a man whose name is unknown, from his person.
JAMES WESTMORLAND ( police-constable F 147.) On the 1st of April I was at Greenwich fair—I saw the prisoner put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, take out a white pocket handkerchief, and put it into his own right-hand trowsers' pocket—I took him into custody, and went to inform the gentleman, but the crowd made a rush, which sent me two or three yards away, and I lost the gentleman—I do not know him—I found the hand-kerchief in the prisoner's pocket—it is marked "W. B."
Prisoner. I picked the handkerchief up, when he caught hold of my collar, and said, "You are the chap I have been looking for." Witness. I saw him put his hand into the gentleman's pocket, and take it out—it was quite clean—I found the blade of a knife in his pocket.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18390408-1430]
1430. JOHN FLOYD was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Susan Champtaloup, on the 21st of March, and stealing therein 1 cap, value 1 s., her property.
SUSAN CHAMPTALOUP . I keep a shop in Bridge-street, Greenwich. On the 21st of March, about a quarter past seven o'clock in the evening, the witness M'Carthy came and gave me information, in consequence of which I missed a man's cap, which I had seen safe at five o'clock, hanging in the passage, against the parlour door—my street door was shut, and fastened with a catch-lock—it could be opened outside—you must come through the passage to get to the shop—the shop door was open—that is on the right-hand side of the passage, and the parlour door on the left.
WILLIAM THOMAS M'CARTHY . I am going on for twelve years of age. I have known the prisoner about six months—on the 21st of March, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, I went to my grandmother's, who lodges I at the prosecutrix's, and saw the prisoner, with two other boys, in the passage, taking the cap—I was standing on the stairs at the time—I had opened the street door myself and gone up—I shut the street door after me—as I went up stairs I heard somebody come in, and heard the bell ring, which hung behind the door—I looked behind me, and saw the prisoner taking the cap down—there was no light in the passage, but there was a lamp outside, and I saw him by that—I saw his face—he passed the cap down to the other boys in the passage, and they ran out into the street—I went after them, and stood and looked at them—they went by a little bow window, and tried it on—the prisoner said it would fetch 6 d.—one of them stopped at the window, while the prisoner and another went with a pair of shoes into Mrs. Champtaloup's shop, and asked her if she would buy them—she said, "No"—I went and asked her if she had lost any thing, and told her what I had seen—I am quite sure I shut the door when I came in—it has a spring lock, and I heard the spring go—I am certain I put my hand to it, and felt it was fast.
DANIEL REARDON . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody on the 21st of March, about seven o'clock in the evening, at the shop of Mr. Wolfe, in Greenwich market—he had this cap in his hand, and another lad was standing at the counter—I asked the prisoner what he was
going to do with it—he raid, "To sell it"—I asked where be got it from—he said from a lad, who was to give him 2 d. to sell it for him.
ELIAS WOOLFE . I keep a shop at Greenwich. The prisoner came to me on the 21st of March, with another boy, and offered the cap for sale—the policeman came and took him.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. A lad sent me to sell it out of the market, and said he would give me two pence—I went to Woolfe—I met a boy going there about a coat, and went in with him-Woolfe offered me sixpence for the cap—I did not sell it to him.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 15. —Recommended to mercy. Transported for Seven Years—Isle of Wight.
Before Mr. Justice Little dale.
[trialAccount t18390408-1431]
1431. RICHARD FOWLER was indicted for feloniously assaulting James. Davidson, on the 17th of March, putting him, in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 1 purse, value 3 d.; 1 crown, 3 half-crowns, 1 shilling, and 7 halfpence; his property.
JAMES DAVIDSON . I am a shipwright, and live at Deptford. On Sunday morning, the 17th of March, I was at the Crown and Mason's Arms, Woolwich, having a glass of rum and milk—the prisoner was there—he was a stranger to me—I asked him to drink with me—he said he did not like rum and milk, he would sooner have beer—I called for a pot of beer, and he drank with me—we then went to the Crown and Cushion—I got some steaks, as he said he was hungry, and he partook of them—there was another man with him—we walked up the town, and came back to the house we had first been to, I think and had a pot of beer or half-and-half there—the other man did not go there with us-every thing was had at my expense—I had come over to Woolwich to see a friend-while at the public-house I had occasion to go backwards, and while in the act of looking at my money in the privy, the prisoner came in and snatched my purse out of money in the privy, the prisoner came in and snatched my ling, and three pence in copper—I said, "You are not going to rob me don't take my money"—he laughed at me, put his hand into the purse, took what he chose, returned me the purse with half-a-crown and one shilling, and went away—he had seen me with the money before I left the room—a woman came in, saying the constable was after him about some robbery, and to get out of the way—he turned out of the house-the constable came, and I gave him in charge—I we not at all tipsy—I did not give him the money to take care of—he asked me to lend him a shilling before I went into the yard, and I said I thought he had had quite sufficient without my lending him any money.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not see you at the Marquis of Granby public-house the first thing that morning, having a glass of rum-and-water? A. You might have seen me there—I was there having a glass of rum—I had two glasses of rum and milk at the Crown and Mason's Arms before you were in my company, and then a pot of beer—we then went to the Crown and Cushion, and had some half-and-half with one or two more, who you asked to drink with you, and I believe we had some rum and spruce—I do not recollect having any half-and-half at the Duke on Horseback-nor at the Standard-nor three pots of half-and-half and some gin at the
Crown—I did not give you my purse to take care of—I do not know how many persons were in the tap-room when I went into the yard—I did not say the morning after you were taken, that if I had not been tipsy I should not have given you in charge—nothing of the kind—I was not tipsy—I gave you in charge the moment I could get out of the yard you were taken close to the place.
JOSEPH BUTTERFILL . I am a constable of Woolwich. On Sunday morning, the 17th of March, the prosecutor came to me—I went with him to the Duke on Horseback, but the prisoner was not there—we went to the Marquis of Gran by, and there found him—I said, "Fowler, you are my prisoner"—he said, "Your prisoner, for what?"—I said, "I will tell you when we get to the door"—when we got to the door, I told him it was for robbing a man of some money—he denied it, and said, "Let go of me, Butterfill, don't lug me through the streets in this sort of way"—I said, "Well, Dick, if you will go quietly I will let you"—I let go of him, and he walked by my side twenty or thirty yards towards the cage—be then buttoned up hi coat, stepped back, and put himself into an attitude to fight—I had great difficulty in keeping his blows off—the prosecutor who was with me, came to my assistance, and the prisoner began sparring with him—we took him to the cage, searched him, and in his jacket pocket found two half-crowns, two penny-pieces, two halfpence, a knife, and a tobacco-box—be told me that Davidson had given him the purse and money to take care of, but seeing him so free in spending the money, he (Davidson) wished it back again—Davidson appeared to have been drinking, but knew what he was about.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not say he asked me for some of it again? A. No, not some of it—you said he asked for it again.
JAMES DAVIDSON re-examined. I have every reason to Believe this money is mine—I know the prisoner had no money while I was in his company, for he asked me to lend him a shilling.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not tell you I had 18 d. of my own? A. No—I lent you the tobacco-box to take a pipe of tobacco from—I do not accuse you of stealing that.
( The prisoner in his defence stated that the prosecutor had been to a great number of public-houses with him, drinking; and being very much intoxicated, had given him his money to take care of for him.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18390408-1432]
1432. JAMES PEW and HENRY JONES were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of James Clamp, from his person.
JAMES CLAMP . I was at Greenwich fair on Tuesday, the 2nd of April, standing, looking at a show—a witness told me my pocket was picked—I turned round and saw both the prisoners in custody of Dyke—I went op to them, and saw my handkerchief drop from under Pew's coat, which was buttoned up—it fell down in front of him—I saw his hand up at his coat at the time it fell—Dyke took it up—I had felt in my pocket, and missed it as soon as the man spoke to me—I knew it to be mine directly I saw it Pew. Q. Did you see my hand in your pocket? A. No, but I saw the hand-kerchief drop from your coat—I did not tell the Magistrate I saw it in your hand—I said, in the officer's hand—I said it was in the prisoner's hand, but immediately corrected myself, and said it was in the officer's hand.
WILLIAM DYKE . I saw the prosecutor at the fair, standing, looking at Richardson's show—the prisoners were behind him, close to him—I saw Pew speak to Jones, and saw Pew take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket in about two minutes—I had not seen Jones do any thing, but he was in company with Pew—I took Pew into custody directly, and the handkerchief dropped from under his coat—I had lost sight of him for a moment—I took it up, and told him I wanted him for stealing the handkerchief from the prosecutor—he said he knew nothing about it, it must have been put under his coat—I am sure he took it—I saw his hand in the pocket—I took them both into custody, with assistance.
Pew. The other witness told him, and he came and collared me. Witness. A young man had called my attention to them—I had watched them for ten minutes.
Jones. Q. What distance were you behind when you saw Pew speak to me? A. Close to you—I did not tell the Magistrate I was some distance behind—there was nobody behind you at all—I told the Magistrate I saw you speak together.
COURT. Q. Did you not say before the Magistrate, "I am quite sure Jones attempted to pick the prosecutor's pocket, I saw him do so?" A. No—that is a mistake on the Magistrate's part in the deposition, not on my part.
GEORGE RANDALL . I am a labourer. I was at Greenwich fair on the 2nd of April, in front of Richardson's show, standing behind Clamp—the two prisoners came up in company together, pushed me away, and pushed in between me and Clamp—I saw Jones wink at Pew, and Jones put his hand to Clamp's pocket to feel it—I watched them a few moments, and seeing they intended to pick the pocket, I turned my head to look for an officer—I went and spoke to Dyke, and went to my place again behind the prosecutor—I saw Pew try to pick the pocket, but he did not do so then Jones was standing close to his side—I stood a few moments longer, and then saw Pew draw the handkerchief out of Clamp's pocket, make a rush on one side, and shove it up his coat—the officers then collared them, and 1 informed Clamp.
Jones. Q. Are you positive you saw us come up there together? A. Yes—the crowd was quite thin when you came up—I saw you attempt the pocket—I was standing close behind you, and could see your arms and hands, and I made a stoop down to look.
Pew. Q. Did not you tell the Magistrate Jones took the handkerchief; and gave it to me? A. No.
Pew's Defence. I am a poor man out of work—going along I saw something like a fair—I went, and stood there with my hands in my pocket looking over the prosecutor's shoulder—there was a great shove and pushing about—I was nearly pushed over another man—I felt a hand go up my coat, but who it was and what they put there I could not see—and before I could do so the policeman charged me with this.
Jones's Defence. It is impossible the witness could have seen me attempt the man's pocket through two persons standing close together—I never saw Pew before.
PEW*— GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Fifteen Years. JONES— NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18390408-1433]
1433. ELIZABETH WOOTTEN was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of April, 1 gown, value 3 s.; and 1 blanket, value 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Wootten. MARIA WOOTTEN . I am the wife of Thomas Wootten, a Greenwich pensioner—the prisoner is my husband's son's wife, and lived with us for nothing—her husband did not live with her—he allowed her 3 s. a week About half-past five o'clock last Monday morning I went out to work leaving my gown and blanket at home—I returned at half-past seven o'clock in the evening, and missed them—I never allowed the prisoner to pawn them.
EDWARD CARTER . I am a pawnbroker at Greenwich. On Monday, the 8th of April, the prisoner pledged this gown and blanket with me—not both at the same time—I believe she pledged the blanket first, the gown next, and she came a third time for more money on the gown—she pledged them in her own name—I knew her very well—I believe a woman named Cook was with her when she pledged the gown.
ROBERT DUNN ( police-constable R 9.) I received information last Monday, and found the prisoner at a house in Queen-street, Greenwich—I told her I took her on suspicion of stealing a gown and blanket—she said she knew nothing at all about it, it was the first she had heard of it—on our way to the station-house I told her I knew where the gown was—she said, "I know where it is too, but the other woman knew nothing at all about it, for I pawned it myself'—I said, "If you pawned them you must know where the duplicates are"—she said, "I have got them here, here they are"—they were pinned to her gown under her shawl—I took them from her, and produce them.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I did not pledge the gown—it was the prosecntrix's son, because his mother had locked up the tea and sugar, and he could not get any breakfast—he pledged it for 8 d., and I went and got 10 d. more on it afterwards—I pledged the blanket myself, and the boy was with me at the time.
EDWARD CARTER re-examined. I am sure it was the prisoner pledged them—the boy had pledged the gown before—I have no recollection of seeing him that day—I will not positively say he was not there—I am positive I saw the prisoner twice about the gown, and she had two separate payments on it—the last payment was in the evening—I cannot say the boy did not pledge it—I am positive the prisoner pledged the blanket—she has been with the boy when he has pledged the gown before.
MRS. WOOTTEN re-examined. I have sent my son to pawn the gown several times before, but I never knew that the prisoner went with him.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18390408-1434]
1434. ANN HEARN was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously assaulting Thomas Hearn, on the 24th of March, and cutting and wounding him upon his face and left cheek, with intent to maim and disable him.—2nd COUNT, stating her intent to be to him some grievous bodily harm.
THOMAS HEARN . I am a labourer, and live in Mill-lane, Deptford—the prisoner is my wife. On Sunday, the 24th of March, about twelve o'clock, I went home to my lodging—I had been drinking at a public-house
in Broadway, Deptford, and was very much intoxicated—my wife was not at home when I got there—when she came in, I began to quarrel with her, through a little jealousy—I accused her of having been drinking with a young man—we came to high words and blows—I struck her and she struck me—we both had a fight together—I was striking a light, and it went out—the candlestick was on the floor, and the candle smashed—I do not know whether the wind blew it out, or whether I and my wife knocked it out—I have given an account of this before—it fell, and went out—I do not know whether I put it out or my wife, for I was very much in liquor—we both had a quarrel together and blows—something struck me on my left arm—I found my face was cut, and ran up stairs for assistance—there were people in the house—I do not know what my arm was cut by—there was no one in the room but our two selves—I must have struck her first, for I am very bad when I am in liquor—I am sure I struck her first, coming in at the door—I think I called her bad names—I did not perceive my hand scratched in the dark—I did not feel the blood before the people told me up stairs that my hand was bleeding.
Prisoner, He caught me by the hair of my head, and threw me on the bed, while I had the knife in my hand. Witness, Very likely I might have done it—I know I was wicked enough to do it—I did not do it to my knowledge, but I was very much in liquor, and do not recollect any thing about it at all—there had been some meat and bread on the table—I did not see any then—I did not see any knife in her hand before the light wat blown out.
Prisoner. Q. Was not I cutting some wood for the fire at the time of the quarrel? A. I saw her cutting some wood, and recollect throwing her down on the ground—both of us were down on the ground—some crockery-ware was broken, and we were fighting amongst it—I do not know about her cutting wood—I accused her wrongfully, and pulled her in-doort—I went to work two days afterwards—I have only had the wound dressed two or three times—I was at work two days afterwards in the rain.
WILLIAM MARSHALL ( police-constable R 187.) I was on duty in Broad-way, Deptford, between twelve and one o'clock on Sunday night, or Monday morning, the 24th, and heard cries of as Police," in Mill-lane—I went down the lane, and met the prosecutor, led by a young man—he appeared to be severely wounded—his face was cut—he was holding his hand to his face—it was bleeding profusely—I directed he should be taken to the station-house, and got surgical assistance—I went with another policeman in search of the prisoner, and found her concealed among some timber and bricks, on the premises of Mr. Mackey, the engineer, adjoining the house where this happened—there is a fence separating the two premises, but that was broken down—it was at the back of the house—she said she had done it, and was quite willing to die, provided she could see him die before her—she did not mention any thing about the gallows—this is my hand-writing—( looking at his deposition)—I believe it was read over to me before I signed it—we searched the room where they were, and my sergeant found this knife, concealed on a shelf in the cupboard—as soon as it was produced she said that was the knife she did it with.
WILLIAM CROXON ( police-sergeant R 20.) On Monday morning, about twenty minutes before one o'clock, I was on duty in Mill-ane—I heard a wrangling from a house up a turning in the lane where the prosecutor and his wile lodged—I saw the prosecutor take a light from the policeman
at the gate—that was directly after I heard the wrangling—he then went in-doors—I remained at the end of the alley—Hearn came out again and asked for a light, as he said Nancy had put it out—I went back with him, and heard the prisoner say, "You b I should like to cut your b throat"—the door was shut—I said "Open the door; are you not ashamed of making use of such language on Sunday night?" Hearn was outside with me—she let him in—I begged of Hearn not to quarrel, and to have no more words—I shut the window-shutter to, being outside, and Hearn fastened it within—about ten minutes afterwards I was in the Fountain yard, about five hundred yards off, and in consequence of what Marshall told me, I went with him to apprehend the prisoner—I found her in Mr. Mackey's yard, about one hundred and fifty yards from the house—Marshall found her at the furthest corner of the yard—there was a quantity of fellies and pieces of timber, to conceal her, and a cart likewise—she had hid herself—she could not be seen without going up to the timber, and looking among it—I afterwards found the knife on a shelf in the cupboard—I think it was the top-shelf—directly I produced it I said, "Is this the knife?"—she said "Yes"—I did not hear distinctly what she said when Marshall took her, not so at to state any particular word.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I come out and say, "I am here, if I have done any thing wrong, I am ready to go with you?" A. She would not come out when Marshall went to where she was, not before he discovered her—she said, "I will go with you"—that was when she was close to me—the cupboard had no door, it was a recess with shelves—I have the shirt here which Hearn had on.
EDWARD DOWNING . I am a surgeon of Deptford. Sergeant Croxon brought Hearn to my house, between one and two o'clock on Monday morning—I examined him—he was bleeding very profusely—he had a wound on his left cheek, about five inches and a half in length, in a half circle, going from the ear to the mouth—one part of it at the middle was to the bone—it was not generally deep—I applied pressure to stop the hemorrhage—being early in the morning I was very much fatigued, having been up the whole night before, and with great difficulty I stopped it—about seven o'clock the same morning the prosecutor called me up again, and I was then obliged to make use of ligatures, as it was bleeding afresh—he was faint—I was obliged to give him a stimulant while I was dressing it—it was certainly a dangerous wound, and if it had been a little lower, I think it would have been fatal—it appeared to be inflicted by a sharp instrument—the knife produced was likely to have done it—I consider it dangerous, if it had not been attended to immediately—he is convalescent, and there is not any fear now.
Prisoner's Defence. After I went home, he began to ill-use me, and caught hold of me by the hair of my head, and tore all my hair out on one side—I had the knife in my hand, having been cutting some wood with it to lay for fire in the morning—he chucked me on the bed, and got his two hands round my neck, trying to choke me—as I was trying to get up again in the struggle, I cut him about the face with the knife, but did nut intend to do so.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Two Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
