1439. HENRY JONES, HENRY UGE , and JOHN WILKINS , were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of March, 6 pairs of leather half-fronts for boots, value 9 s., the goods of Henry Crossley.
JAMES CROSSLEY . I am the son of Henry Crossley, of Church-street, Deptford. On the 26th of March, the three prisoners came to his shop Jones came in first for a pair of leather half-fronts, and while he was looking at them, Uge came in and bought some small articles which came to 3 d. or 4 d., which he paid for, and went out—as soon as he went out Wilkins came for some things which came to 8 1/2 d —he paid, and as soon
as he went Jones paid half-a-crown for a pair of fronts, and went away—I then put the fronts away, and thought some were missing—I went to the door and saw the three prisoners together—I spoke to a policeman—they saw me and ran away—they turned into Effingham-place, and one of them threw a bag down—we followed them, I suppose a mile, and took all three of them in the Creek-road—a boy took up the bag, and I gave it to the policeman—it contained eleven pairs of half-fronts—six pairs of them belonged to my father.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Has your father any partner? A. No—I will undertake to say that Wilkins was in the shop while Jones was there—he bought some hand shank, and a quarter of a yard of Union, which came to 81/2 d.—I am certain he did not buy any thing else—Uge and him were not in the shop together—I never said they were—Wilkins paid me with a half-crown, and had 1 s. 91/2 d. change—I observed nothing about him.
JAMES GATES ( police-constable R 144.) I had a communication from Crossley—the prisoners were all three running together—I ran and followed them till they ran out of my sight—I understood which way they went, and cut a nearer way—I met them in the Creek-road—Wilkins then ran from the other two—I pursued and took him in the Stowage—I did not see the bag till I took them to the station-house—I then had it from Mr. Crossley.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where did you see them first? A. In Church-street, about one hundred and fifty yards from the prosecutor's shop—I cannot say whether either of them had a bag at that time—they are the same parties that had passed me before Mr. Crossley spoke to me.
WILLIAM JAMES MORGAN . I am the son of the Deptford carrier. About twelve o'clock on the 26th of March, I was at the corner of Effingham-place—I saw a cart there, and a man putting a horse in it—these three men ran by—one of them threw a blue bag away, and it lodged in the cart—this is the bag—I can swear to it—it was like this bag—I ran after the mob —I did not pick it up.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Howmany were in the mob? A. About forty were following after them.
COURT to JAMES CROSSLEY. Q. Who found the bag? A. The boy showed me where it was, and I took it out of a coal cart which was standing at the corner of Effingham-place.
GEORGE WILSON ( police-constable R 179.) Uge was given into my custody—I found on him what he had bought at the shop.
NATHANIEL LEIGH . I do not live with the prosecutor, but the prisoners Jones and Wilkins came to my master's shop before this day.
JOSEPH HALL . The three prisoners were at my father's shop on the Saturday before.
(William Cannon, shoemaker, and James Marriott, hair-dresser, of Blue Anchor-yard, gave Uge a good character; Barnett Quin, butcher, of Stoney-lane; Edward Ferne, carpenter, Swan-street, Minories; and Abraham Green, gave Jones a good character.) JONES— GUILTY . Aged 25.
UGE*— GUILTY . Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years. WILKINS— GUILTY . Aged 22.
(There was another indictment against Jones and Wilkins.)

1440. MARIA FORSTER was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of March, 1 purse, value 6 d.; 3 sovereigns, and 3 shillings, the goods and monies of Joseph Reading, from his person.
JOSEPH READING . I am an engineer. I am fifty-seven years old—I went to Woolwich on Good Friday morning—I had something to eat, and got too much drink—I was coming home, and turned down the lane by New Cross—the prisoner followed me—we went down the lane together—I missed my purse, in which I had three sovereigns and some silver, from my left hand pocket—I had seen the money safe a little before I went down the lane—I was rather disguised in liquor—I told the prisoner she had robbed me—she said she had not touched me, nor touched my pros perty—this is my purse, it has three sovereigns in it.
Prisoner. You asked me to go down the lane. Witness. I did not—we had not been drinking for three hours together—the policeman was not coming after me for passing a bad sixpence.
JOHN ROSCOE ( police-constable R 119.) I was on duty near New Cross—I saw the prosecutor and the prisoner walk down Five Bell-lane—I followed, and when I got near them, the prosecutor told me she had robbed him of his purse and money—the prisoner said, "I have neither seen purse nor money, so help me God"—I took her to the station-house.
MART WALKER . I am searcher at the station-house, I found this purse and money concealed on the prisoner's person.
Prisoner's Defence, I was walking down the lane—he fell down, and was very tipsy—he had this purse in his hand—I helped him up, and he dropped it into my hand.

1441. THOMAS POPKINS, EDWARD COOK , and THOMAS CASSIDY , were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of April, 110lbs. weight of iron, value 5 s., the goods of the London and Greenwich Railway Company.
MARY ANN SMITH . I am the wife of Charles Smith, and live in Church-street, Deptford, and deal in marine stores. On the 2nd of April, the prisoners came to my house together—each one brought a bar of iron—they appeared to me to belong to the rail-road—I asked whose iron it was—they said Mr. Cook's—I said if Mr. Cook came himself I would buy it—they told me that Mr. Cook was outside—they went to call him and ran away, leaving the iron in my shop—the policeman came and took it next morning.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you quite sure there was not one outside? A. Yes, they were all inside.
JAMES BARRBLL ( police-sergeant R 8.) From information I apprehended Popkins on Friday morning—I asked him who the others were who were with him, when he took the iron to Mrs. Smith's—he said Cook and Custody, and he should not have taken it if they had not persuaded him.
THOMAS WATSON . I am an officer of the London and Greenwich Rail-road Company. I have the Act of Parliament constituting the Company here—I have seen this iron, it is ours—there is but one other rail-road that has a rail of this description—theirs is a quarter of an inch lower than this.
The document produced by the witness as the Act of Parliament, not having the name of the Queen's printer affixed, the prisoners were ACQUITTED .

1443. ANN CARR was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 121/2lbs. weight of butter, value 10 s.; 1 1/2 lb. weight of bacon, value 1 s.; 111/2lbs. weight of cheese, value 2 s. 2 d.; and 9 eggs, value 6 d.; the goods of John Nelson, her master: and JOHN MOORE , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM CROWLEY ( police-constable R 178.) In consequence of information, I went, on the 5th of April, about a quarter-past six o'clock in the morning, and placed myself near Mr. Nelson's house—I stood at the top of New King-street, Deptford—I saw the prisoner Moore come down Broomfield-place with a basket in his hand—he turned down Old Kings street, and stopped at the back door of Mr. Nelson's premises—he remained there about a minute or two—I saw the gate opened, and he went into the yard—I went and stood two or three yards below the gate—I could not see into the yard—I remained two or three minutes, when I saw the gate opened—Moore came out with a basket in one band, and a small bundle in the other—(the front of Mr. Nelson's shop was not open at that time)—Moore saw me, and appeared very much confused—he turned round, and walked into the yard again quickly—I followed, and about t yard inside the gate I took him by the arm, and told him, from information I had received, I should take him into custody on suspicion of robbing Mr. Nelson—the female prisoner was standing about a yard from him at the time—they both appeared very much confused—on the way to the station-house, Moore repeatedly begged me to let him go, if not, it would ruin him—he said the things were given him by a female—the basket contained twelve pounds and a half of butter, and the bundle a Dutch cheese, a piece of Gloucester cheese about half a pound, a piece of bacon weighing about a pound and a half, and in his great-coat pocket were nine eggs, which he took out—I went to Mr. Nelson's house—Mr. Nelson and and I went to Moore's lodgings, and, in a cupboard in a room occupied by Moore, I found a small piece of Cheshire cheese, which Mr. Nelson identified—about nine o'clock the same morning I took Carr in Pleasant-row—she told me she had given the things to Moore.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Carr is sister to the prosecutor, is not she? A. I believe she is his sister-in-law—the prosecutor's back gate is in Old King-street—it opens into the yard which is connected with the houses—I saw Moore go into the yard, and he remained there two or three minutes at furthest—I cannot say whether he entered the house—I could not see him in the yard, as the gate was shut.
JOHN NELSON . I am a cheese monger, and live in New King-street, Deptford. Carr is my wife's sister, and was in my service—on the morning of the 5th of April the policeman came to my house before I was up—in consequence of what he said I went to Moore's house, and found some eggs in the cupboard, twelve in a basin and two in a dish; three small pieces of cheese weighing about half a pound, and nearly a pound of butter in a plate—I had cheese resembling what I found, but I could not swear
it was mine—there is a door between my shop and the parlour, which has no lock on it, but it is always shut at night—my butter was kept in the shop—I believe the butter found on the prisoner Moore to be mine, and I have more Dutch cheese similar to this one found on Moore—I cannot say that I missed butter, as my stock is large, and I have a son who manages my business—I am sometimes out for two or three hours—Carr had been with me sixteen years—I never saw Moore in my house—he has been to the door to inquire for Carr—I understood he paid his addresses to her.
Cross-examined. Q. You have a wife alive, I suppose? A. Yes, and I have a son in the business—I believe the butter to be mine—I have some of the same kind—I have not missed any cheese or eggs.
CARR— GUILTY . Aged 39.— Confined One Month.
MOORE. NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1444]

1445. HENRY PALMER and RICHARD HALL were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOAN I conducted the Prosecution. THOMAS HAWES WARREN. I keep a chandler's-shop, in Carrterbury-plaee, Old Kent-road. On the 16th of March, about four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner Hall came into my shop-for one penny-worth of tobacco—he put down a bad shilling—I tried it, and it was bad—I bit it, and bent it, and gave it to him back—he went away—I directed two of my sons to follow him, and tell a policeman.
THOMAS WARREN . I am the prosecutor's son. My father told me to go after Hall—my brother was with me—when Hall got as for as the Crown and Anchor public-house, he crossed the road, and then threw a tobacco pipe at Palmer, and another man, who got away—they walked on some distance, and then the other one went into a baker's shop—I went into the baker's—I did not see the prisoners taken.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you hear the other's name? A. Smith was the name he gave at the station-house.
ROBERT WARRBN . I went out with my brother to watch Hall—he went on towards the Crown and Anchor—I then saw Palmer, and the other man—they approached together, and went on talking about 200 yards, then the other left them, and went into a baker's shop, and the two prisoners went on on the other side of the way—when the little one came out of the baker's shop, he went on eating a roll—he went on the same side as the baker's—he did not join the prisoners again—my brother sent me on to follow Hall and Palmer—I did so, and saw three policemen—I told them something, and they went in pursuit of the prisoners—when the policeman had been spoken to, I saw Palmer look round, and then run—Hall ran after him—they ran in the same direction, and as they ran I saw Hall hold up his hand, and throw something over Mr. Cormack's garden-wall—there is a little bend in the wall there, and it was close at the bend that he threw it.
JOHN ROSCOE ( police-constable R 119.) I met this witness, and in consequence of what he said, I and the policemen went in pursuit of the prisoners—while they were running I saw Palmer pass some-thing to Hall—it appeared to he something in a white paper—I then called, "Stop thief,"—a gentleman collared Hall, and I saw Hall hold up his hand, as if to throw something over the wall—I did not see any thing thrown—I pursued, and took the prisoners—I found on Hall three farthings and a tobacco-box.
Cross-examined. Q. Must you not have seen if there had been any thing thrown? A. I was running very swiftly, and perhaps I did not take particular notice—I observed him put up his arm—I saw Palmer look back before they began to run—Hall might have thrown something away, and I not see it.
JAMES ERRINGTON . I am a laborer, and live at Peck ham. I was returning from Greenwich, on the evening of the 16th of March—I was opposite Mr. Cormack's wall, and heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw the two prisoners running—Palmer was rather a-head of Hall—some gentleman tried to stop Hall, and I saw Hall pull his hand out of his right hand-pocket, and throw some shillings over the wall—I saw them scale over the wall—they ran about fifty yards further, and were stopped.
JOSEPH WILLIAM CLARK ( police-constable R 99.) In consequence of what a gentleman said to me, I went through Mr. Cormack's gate, searched behind the wall, and picked up these twelve shillings.
GEORGE PEDDER ( police-constable R 108.) I searched the same spot, and found five more shillings.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to her Majesty's Mint—these seventeen shillings are all counterfeit, and all cast in the same mould.
PALMER— GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined One Year.
HALL— GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.

1448. THOMAS HOY was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 4 pairs of boots, value 1 l. 2 s., the goods of William Freeman.
WILLIAM FREEMAN . I am a shoemaker, and live in Greenwich-road. I was sitting in my back parlour on the 10th of April, and in consequence of something ray wife told me I ran out of doors—I saw the prisoner rather running—I pursued him, and took hold of his collar—I said, what have you been taking? and at that moment I saw a boot fall from his person—I took it up, and with the boot and the prisoner I proceeded towards the station-house—Mr. Judson my neighbour came up—I wished him to go and see if there were not some more boots—be said, "If you will go I will hold the boy"—I went back, and close to where the prisoner had been standing when I took him, I found seven more boots—they were all mine.

[trialAccount t18390408-1449]
1449. MARY RHODA JONES was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
ELEANOR MART ELIZABETH LOCKER . I am the wife of Edward William Locker; he is one of the Commissioners of Greenwich Hospital. On the 31st of December the prisoner called at my house—she was quite a stranger—she stated that her husband was transported, and she was very anxious to join him—that a ship was lying at Gravesend, and her children were on board, and she was very anxious to raise money to go there—she showed me this card, which she said had been given her by the owners of the ship, and it was to sail very shortly—seeing the card I believed her story, and gave her a crown and some trifling articles—she did not give me the card, bat laid it down and forgot it—I found it after she left.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you kept the card ever since? A. No, I sent it to Mr. Gordon—if this is not the card she produced, it is so like it that I cannot tell the difference—I read it while she was there-rand after she left, and kept it till I sent it to Mr. Gordon—I gave the prisoner a crown-piece as an act of charity and humanity, supposing what she said was correct.
JAMES BRODIE GORDON . I am a merchant, and live in Leaden hall-street. I am owner of the vessel called the Mary Ann —I remember seeing the prisoner, I think it was in October last—the Mary Ann sailed for Hobart Town in November, Henry Marshall was commander—the prisoner called a week or ten days before it sailed—she said she came from a Mr. Palliser, a gentleman on Fins bury-pavement—she wanted a passage to Hobart Town, and as I had a ship sailing he had sent her to me—she stated a pitiful case to me—said her husband was on the other side, and she was very desirous to go with her two children to him—she asked the fare, I said it would be 60 l. or 70 l.; but as she told me such a story I
would put it at one-half of that, and she should go in a cabin by herself and her two children for 35 l.—I gave her this card—she said she would go and get the money, that she had got 24 l. in the hand of a friend in the City, and she would bring me that in the course of a quarter of an hour—I told her the vessel would sail in a few days, and it was then lying in the London Docks—I did not see her again—her children were never on board the vessel to my knowledge—the next I heard of her was by a letter from Mrs. Locker inclosing this card, which I had given the prisoner—the ship was at Gravesend one day, on her passage.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you receive this letter from Mrs. Locker personally? A. No, by post—I had no other vessel beside the Mary Ann going that month—I had a vessel sailed in January to Sydney, not to Hobart Town.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you got the letter you received which in closed the card? A. Yes—I had no conversation with the prisoner about any other vessel than the Mary Ann.
MRS. LOCKER. This is the letter I sent to Mr. Gordon with the card In closed.
ALICE YOUNG . The prisoner lived in the same house that I do in James-street, New-cut, Lambeth, from September till she was taken in custody—a man of the name of Thompson lived in the same place with her—she had two children, who went to school with my little girl—they are now in Lambeth workhouse—I don't know what has become of Thompson.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you ever hear of her having a husband transported? A. I have heard the children say so.
WILLIAM HORSFORD . I am constable to the Mendicity Society—I have known the prisoner about four years—I was sent for by the inspector on the 7th or 8th of March—the prisoner was then in custody on another charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you examined before the Magistrates? A. No, I was ordered to attend here—I don't know any thing of this case.
(The prisoner delivered in a petition for a lenient sentence, stating herself to be in a destitute state.)
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

[trialAccount t18390408-1451]
1451. WILLIAM BRAGG was indicted for embezzlement.
MARY ANN DOWLEY . I am the wife of William Edward Downey, a baker, at Upper Tooting. The prisoner lived with us about twelve months as journeyman—he was entrusted to receive money on his masters account for bread which he took out—he ought to account for the money he received the same day—he did not account for the receipt of any
money on the 28th of March from Mrs. Newbury, who is a customer of ours—we had suspicions the night he received this 8 s.—he was asked for it, and denied having it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was your husband unwell? A. Yes—the prisoner did not manage the business—he did not make the bread—he ought to have paid the 8 s. on Thursday—he was taken in custody on the Saturday—there was a balance of 3 s. 6 d. then due to him, not a week's wages—he was not in the habit of deducting his wages from the money he received—I was not always present when he settled with my husband—he was going away a month before, and we increased his wages 1 s. a-week, I think, to stay.
CHARLOTTE NEWBURT . I live at Lower Tooting. I dealt with the prosecutor for bread which the prisoner brought—on the 28th of March I paid him 8 s. on his master's account, between twelve and one o'clock in the day—he receipted the bill—it was 9 s., but there was a mistake in it of 1 s., which he took off.
WILLIAM PEARSON . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner at the prosecutor's house, on Saturday the 30th of March—I told told I took him for receiving Mrs. Newbury's bill—he said he had received it, and if his master would allow him a little time, he would contrive to make the money up.
JURY to MRS. DOWLEY. Q. Do you know whether he had received say other money on his master's account the same day as the 8 s.? A. Not to my knowledge—we had overlooked other things which he had committed before?
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 21. —Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
[trialAccount t18390408-1452]
1452. JAMES JEFFRIES was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Bond, at Bermondsey, about the hour of three in the night of the 1st of March, with intent to steal, and stealing therein 1 printed book, value 4 s.; 2 snuff-boxes, value 15 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; 1 sheet, value 2 s.; 1 pair of shoes, value 4 s.; 1 table-doth, value 1 s.; 1 frock, value 2 s.; and 1 pair of gloves, value 6 d. his goods.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
HARRIET BOND . I am the wife of Henry Bond of George's-terrace, Grimscott-street, Bermondsey. On Friday, the 1st of March, I was the last person up—I went to bed at twelve o'clock—I examined the house, and it was all fast—I locked the kitchen window—the shutters were not closed—that window looks against a dead wall on the back premises, and it about two yards from the wall—I was disturbed about three o'clock, as my youngest girl was taken ill, and crying, and the dog was barking—I did not go down stairs then—I heard nothing more particular in the night—I got up soon after six o'clock next morning, went down into the kitchen, and found the window-sash half open, and a pane of glass over the fastening broken—it was whole and fastened the night before—a person could put something through the broken pane to open the fastening, but the space was not large enough to admit a hand through—the sash could then be lifted up—I went into the front parlour, and missed "Burkitt's Notes on the New Testament," a pair of shoes, a pair of gloves, a silk handkerchief,
and two snuff-boxes—one was a painted one, and the other embossed with the Holy Family on the lid—I missed from the kitchen a table-cloth a sheet, and a child's frock—I had seen them all safe the evening before.
HENRY NOAKES . I live with my mother in Unicorn-square, Tooley. street I hawk papers about, and do any thing I can get—I know the prisoner—on Saturday, the 2nd of March, between two and three o'clock in the afternoon, I saw him at the Eight Bells and Anchor public-house in Bermondsey-street—he showed me a book, a kind of Testament, and asked me to pledge it for him—I opened it, and saw no hard names in it as I had always seen in Matthew—I pawned it at Mr. Rosier's in Tooley. street for 2 s.—I gave the prisoner the money and the duplicate—be said I was better known at the shop than him, and he would stand a pint of beer if I did—he gave me a share of a pint of beer.
HENRY NEWSTEAD . I am shopman to Mr. Rosier, a pawnbroker, in Tooley-street. I produce a book which was pawned by Noakes on the 2nd of March—it is "Burkitt on the New Testament"—the first page of Matthey is out—I have also some sleeves and a frock, pawned by the prisoner on the afternoon of the 2nd of March for 4 s., also a sheet, a shirt, and a pair of shoes at the same time.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not say at the office you could not swear to me? A. That was respecting a silk pelisse which was pawned—I an positive you pawned these things.
GEORGE HAVILL ( police-sergeant M 2.) I took the prisoner in charge on the 5th of March at the Surrey theatre. I took him to the station-house—I searched him, and found a silk handkerchief round his neck, a pair of gloves in his pocket, and a snuff-box—he said he gave 3 d. for the snuff-box, the gloves he had had eighteen months, and the silk handkerchief was well known to be his own—Noakes was present when I apprehended him—I asked the prisoner how he came in possession of the book he gave Noakes to pawn—he said he gave 1 s. 3 d. or 1 s. 6 d. for it to a sailor, who had gone to Gravesend.
WILLIAM WILKINS ( police-constable R 11.) I went to the station-house and took off the prisoner's shoes, having observed footmarks in Mr. Bond's back premises—I compared the shoes with the footmarks which I had covered over to preserve them, and they corresponded—I made an impression by the side of the footmark—the ground was soft—there were nails in the shoes and also in the impression.
Prisoner. The nails in the shoes could not make any Impression—they are so worn. Witness, They are not worn down so much as the prisoner states.
Prisoner, He said at the cell of the office that I must have worn them a great deal since the robbery. Witness, I did not say a word of the sot.
GEORGE HAVILL re-examined. I was at the cell of the office—there was no such conversation passed.
MRS. BOND re-examined. This is our Testament—here is a misprint which my husband corrected, in my presence—this snuff-box and these gloves are ours—they are the gloves the prisoner was wearing—this hand-kerchief which he wore, is ours—this child's frock is mine, and was worn that day by my child—this sheet was taken out of the kitchen—the table-cloth and one snuff-box have not been found.
Prisoner's Defence, I was going to my master's house on Saturday evening, to see if I might return to work, as he had discharged me—I
passed the Eight Bells, and as I came back, about one o'clock, I met a young sailor chap, with a book and other things in his hand—he asked me to buy it—I gave him 18 d. for the book, and 3 d. for a snuff-box—we went into a public-house, and had a pint of four penny ale—on the Sunday morning he met me, and said he would sell me the duplicate of a handkerchief—I said I did not wish to have it—I met him again on Monday morning, and bought the ticket of him—I gave him 1 s. 3 d. to get it out, and wore it, and afterwards he gave me the gloves.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
(There were other indictments against the prisoner.)
Before Mr. Justice Littledale,
[trialAccount t18390408-1453]
1453. CATHERINE DENNY was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously, assaulting Bridget Santry, on the 15th of March, and cutting and wounding her on the head, the left side of her face, and left hand, with intent to maim and disable her—2nd COUNT, stating her intent to be to do her some grievous bodily harm.
BRIDGET SANTRY . I am the wife of Dennis Santry, and live at Rotherhithe, in the same house as the prisoner, and Margaret Mulchannaugh. About half-past seven o'clock in the morning of the 15th of March I was lying on my bed—I heard the prisoner outside at the foot of the stairs, calling me by an expression not fit to mention—in the course of ten minutes I heard her again addressing me with the same kind of expression as before—I went down stairs, and knocked at her door, and told her I wanted to speak to her—the second time I spoke she opened the door, and let me in—I told her she deserved a good hiding for her filthy language to me—she told me to hit her—I said I did not feel myself able to do it, but if I was, she deserved it for her abuse to me—she took bold of my hair with her left hand, took her right hand from behind her back, and I felt her stab me on the left side of my head with a knife, but I did not see the knife till after Mrs. Mulcbannaugh came up and took it out of her hand—I was standing up at the time—directly I saw the blood over me, I got so weak I fell down directly—I had two wounds and a scratch—I put up my left hand in my defence, and it cut through my fingers—I felt pain from the cuts, and was very unwell indeed—I was attended by a surgeon for ten days—the blood-vessel then burst out again, and I sent for the doctor, who is attending me still.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Who went down stairs with you to the prisoner's room? A. No one—I am sure of that—her husband was up stairs in my room, and my husband, and Mrs. Mulchannaugh also—we had half a pint of gin between five of us—it was half-past seven o'clock in the morning—I had not been at work—I was on my bed—I got up when the prisoner came to abuse me—she opened her room door to me the second time I knocked—she was alone in her room—she did not receive any injury in the scuffle, that I know of—she had none from me—there was no one but me there—I saw no blood on her—I have had no money from her or her husband about this, but the husband when I first came to the house, warned me not to give her any rent—I have had a sovereign from him since this, and gave it to the surgeon—he has promised me another sovereign—this was the prisoner's house—I was a lodger—she was not chopping wood in her room, that I saw—there was none thrown at her—the policeman came in about half an hour.
MARGARET MULCHANNAUGH . I am the wife of John Mulchannangh and lodged in the house. On the morning of the 15th of March, I heard the prosecutrix scream out—I went to her—she was standing in the prisoner's room—the prisoner had got hold of her hair by her left hand, and had a carving knife in her right hand—I saw her stab the prosecutrix in the left side of the head, before I had power to take the knife from her—I saw blood come from the prosecutrix—I took the knife from the prisoner and chucked it inside the fender—I had heard the prisoner come to the foot of the stairs, and abuse the prosecutrix as she lay on her bed.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been out that morning? A. Yes, between seven and eight o'clock, for half-a-pint of gin, which we drank in the prosecutrix's room with her husband and the prisoner's husband—the prisoner was below in her own room—I did not hear the prosecutrix say any thing to the prisoner—the policeman came a few minutes after the deed was committed—I cannot say what space it was.
JOHN LONG . I am a policeman. Between seven and eight o'clock in the morning of the 15th of March, I was called into the prisoner's house, and took her into custody—her husband gave me this knife—( producing it)—I asked the prisoner how she came to do it—she said both the witness and the prosecutrix rushed in upon her—that the knife laid on the table, and she laid hold of it to save herself.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you desire the prisoner to wipe her face before taking her to the station house? A. No; but she was bleeding from the forehead—she has the mark now—I did not observe any poker in the room, or any pieces of wood—her face was streaming down with blood—my body must have seen it.
WILLIAM MURDOCK . I am a surgeon, and live in Rotherhithe-street I was called in to see the prosecutrix on the morning of the 15th of March—I found her seated in a corner of the room, her face covered with blood, and her hair dishevelled, falling over her face, clotted with blood—she had fainted from loss of blood, and was then recovering—I found two cuts and a scratch on the left side of her head, a cat on her face, and a cut between the first and second finger of the left hand—they were simple cut wounds—I did not consider them dangerous—they might have been inflicted with this knife—I see a mark on the blade near the handle, which I believe to be blood—I have attended her ever since—she was confined to her bed nine or ten days—she is very near recovery now—there is a wound not quite healed.
MARGARET MULCHANNAUGH re-examined. That is the knife I took out of the prisoner's hands. NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr., Recorder.

1454. JAMES SOMERVTLLE was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Walmsley and others, on the 4 the of April, and stealing therein 1 snuff-box, value 36 s., their property.
JOSEPH BRIDGE . I am shopman to George Walmsley and two other, in High-street, Borough, in the parish of St. George the Martyr, South-work. On the afternoon of the 4th of April, I was in the shop—I heard the window crack, turned towards it, and saw the prisoner's hand inside the window—he took a silver snuff-box out, and went by the window with it—I went out, secured him with it in his hand, and gave him in charge.
MICHAEL MANNING ( police-constable M 173.) I took the prisoner into custody with the box in his hand, and the ticket attached to it.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1455]
1455. DAVID FITZGERALD was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Speller, on the 10th of March, at Christ Church, Surrey, and stealing therein, 1 tea-caddy, value 12 s.; 1 work-box, value 8 s.; 1 table-cloth, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 table-cover, value 10 s.; and 5lbs. weight of beef, value 3 s., his property.
WILLIAM SPELLER . I am an iron-plate worker, and live in Yorkplace, Christ Church, Surrey—I rent the whole house. About a quarter to eight o'clock on the night of the 10th of March, in consequence of information, I went into Thorough-street, which is about one hundred and fifty yards from my house, and saw the prisoner and a lad about fourteen yean old—the prisoner passed me with some articles—I found nobody further on and returned, and then found the prisoner kneeling down—I nearly stepped on him—I looked over his shoulder—he asked me what I was looking at—I said, "Nothing particular"—he said, "You can go on"—I said, "I know that," and as he rose up I collared him, and told him I thought he had something belonging to me—I brought him towards the gas-light, and saw the tea-caddy and work-box shine through the handkerchief—I afterwards found the table cover in William-street, in the road—I gave him in charge.
HARRIET SPELLER . I am the prosecutor's wife. On the night of the 10th of March, I heard a noise in the front-parlour, like the rattling of plates' and dishes—I had noticed the window between two and three o'clock in the afternoon, it was then down—I went into the parlour on bearing. the noise, and found the window and one blind open, and missed from the table a piece of beef, a tea-caddy, a work-box, a table-cloth, and a table-cover—I had fastened the blinds and shut the window down that day, and they remained so till I heard the noise—it would require a light to see into the parlour at eight o'clock—I took a candle with me—there was the appearance of a candle having been on the table, and a lucifer match, and a smell of sulphur.
RICHARD SENIOR . I am a plumber, and live in Lesson Grove. I was spending the evening at Speller's—he brought the prisoner to the house and gave him into my charge, with the work-box and tea-caddy tied in ft handkerchief—he threw them down at my feet—I took them up and gave them to the constable—I had gone to the house about five o'clock, and the window was down then.
ROBERT TURTLB ( police-constable M 56.) On the night of the 10th of March, I was called to the house, and received charge of the prisoner with the things—I found a box of lucifers in his pocket, and Mrs. Speller gave me one partly burnt, off the table.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had only come out of the country on Saturday evening—I had spent the Sunday with a friend—as I came home I saw the smallest box lying down in the middle of Thurlow-street, and about six yards from it was the work-box—I tied them in my handkerchief, and the gentleman came up and took me.
GUILTY . * Aged 18.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1456]
1456. ELIZABETH HAWES was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of March, at Camberwell, 1 box, value 1 d.; 4 sovereigns, 3 half-sovereigns. 2 dollars, and 2 s.; the goods and monies of Flavius Josephus Hudson, her master, in his dwelling-house.
FLAVIUS JOSEPHUS HUDSON . I am a tea-dealer and grocer, and live in High-street, Peckham, in the parish of Camberwell. On Saturday, the 23rd of March, the prisoner came into my service—on the 27th I sent her out, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, on an errand—she did not return—she was apprehended on the Friday following—I searched my bed. room, as she did not return, and missed three sovereigns, three half-sovereigns, two dollars, and two old shillings, out of the looking-glass drawer—three of the sovereigns were in a small box—the drawer was locked, and I found it still locked.
Prisoner. The drawer was not locked.
PETER WALLIS ( police-constable E 72.) I took the prisoner into custody, on the 29th of March, in Vine-court, Tooley-street—I told her the charge—she denied it—I asked if she had any of the property about her person—she said she had not, but that she had part of it stolen from her by a young man, and the remainder she had spent—I found a box on her, which the prosecutor swears to—she had several new clothes on—her clothes were given to me at a house she had taken refuge in—there is a ring and necklace in the bundle.
SARAH EVEREST . I am the wife of David Everest, and live at the station-house, in Lock's-fields. I searched the prisoner, when she was brought to the station-house, and found on her this salve-box, a ring, and a necklace on her neck, which I delivered to the policeman—I asked her what she was charged with—she said robbing her master of six sovereigns, and some silver, but she had not taken more than 4 l. 10 s.—I asked what she did with it—she said she had spent some, and got drunk, and was robbed of some of it.
FLAVIUS JOSEPH HUDSON re-examined. This salve-box I can identify as being the one the sovereigns were in—I am quite sure of it, by a particular mark.
GUILTY . * Aged 17— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18390408-1458]
1458. JAMES BELL was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of December, 5 handkerchiefs, value 22 s., and 6 yards of lawn, value 10 s., the goods of Martin Atkinson, his master; and JEMIMA BELL , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. JONES conducted the Prosecution.
MARTIN ATKINSON . I keep a linen-drapery warehouse, in the Westminster-road. James Bell had been in my service for about eight months, up to March last, and I had known him for some years—I had some suspicion, and directed a shopman to watch him—on the evening of the 7th of March my shopman communicated something to me, and the prisoner was called back, as he was about leaving my premises—he followed me into my
counting-house—I said to him, "Give me back my property"—he pulled a piece of huckaback out of his pocket, and gave it to me—it was my property—he said, "I hope you will for give roe, sir, for the sake of my family, and if you will allow me to go home, I will return to-morrow morning, and then do what you like"—I gave him into custody—I have since seen five black silk handkerchiefs, and a piece of lawn, which are mine—these are them—( examining them)—I had missed the handkerchiefs from my shop, and made an outcry about them—I was present at the examination of the prisoners, they made a statement which was taken down, and as I was leaving the Court, they went up to sign it—this signature to this examination is the hand-writing of James Bell—I do not know the hand-writing of the other prisoner, who is his daughter.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe this man has been in much better life than he is now? A. He was in business for himself about thirteen years back—he has a wife, and I think four children—when he came first to me he had 25 s. a-week, as I had younger hands, who took more money than he did, I intimated that he might leave me, but at be said he wished I would keep him till the turn of the year, I said I would give him 20 s. a-week, which I did—he boarded in my house.
GEORGE WORLEY . I am in the service of Messrs. Walmsley, pawn-brokers. I produce five handkerchiefs and the lawn, which were pawned on the 19th and 22nd of December, I believe, by the prisoner Jemima Bell.
ROBERT OVENDEN ( police-constable L 90.) I attended the examination of the prisoners—I saw them sign this examination—( read)—"Jemima Bell says, I remember pledging the black silk handkerchiefs; my father gave them to me, and told me to pledge them. The man says, My daughter acted under my direction."
JAMES BELL— GUILTY . Aged 54.
JEMIMA BELL— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1459]
1459. JAMES BELL was again indicted for stealing, on the 4th of January, 1 table-cloth, value 4 s., the goods of Martin Atkinson, his master; and SARAH BELL , for feloniously receiving the same, well bowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. JONES conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE THOMPSON . I am shopman to Mr. Russell, a pawnbroker, in Old Kent-road. I produce a table-cloth pawned, on the 4th of January, by Sarah Bell.
MARTIN ATKINSON . This table-cloth is mine—it was never sold to James Bell.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARXSON. Q. You cannot say that it may not have been sold? A. No, nor when it was missed.
ROBERT OVENDON . I saw Sarah Bell sign this deposition—( read)—"Sarah Bell says, I pledged the table-cloth; my father gave it me to pledge. James Bell says, My daughter acted under my direction."
JAMES BELL— GUILTY . Aged 54.— Transported for Seven Years.
SARAH BELL— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1460]
1460. WILLIAM EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 1/4lb. weight of tea, value 1 s.; and 1 brush, value 3 s.; the goods of Henry Craven, his master.
HENRY CRAVEN . I am a grocer, and live in Wandsworth-road The,
prisoner was my shopman—I suspected he was robbing me, and in the beginning of March I marked some money, and gave it to a neighbour to bring to my shop—the prisoner was searched for a shilling, and this tea was found on him, on the morning of the 12th of March—I have tea which exactly resembles this, and the paper it is in exactly resembles mine, but I could not say that I had lost any tea—this brush is mine—it was found in the prisoner's box.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Where was the box? A. Up in his bed-room—it was locked—the key was taken from him by the officer—it is a horse-brush, but it will brush clothes—I never knew him to brush his clothes up stairs—he generally came down into the kitchen to do that—he had been in my service about twelve months.
JAMES WILLIAM BURRIDGE ( police-constable V 160.) I was called in—I found a shilling on the prisoner, and this tea and this brush in his box.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he give you the key of his box? A. Yes—he said nothing about the brush or the tea.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18390408-1461]
1461. WILLIAM EVANS was again indicted for embezzlement.
HENRY CRAVEN . I live in Britannia-place, Wands worth road. On the 11th of March, I marked three shillings, and gave them to Mr. Young, a draper close by, to bring to my shop at half past seven o'clock the next morning, and in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour after Mr. Young was gone, I went to the till, and there was only one of the marked shillings in it—this is one of the shillings—( examining one.)
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. How is it marked? A. In three places with small dots made with a brad-awl—if the prisoner had to give change for any thing, be gave it out of the till—he had no right to give change out of his own money, and I never knew him to do it—in my absence if he could not get change from the till, he might give change from his pocket, but on that morning there was no larger money in the till then shillings—I usually take the money out of the till at night—I cannot tell how many customers had been there—there were five or six shillings beside the marked shilling—there is 14 s. due to him for two weeks' wages—I boarded him.
CHARLES YOUNG . On the 11th of March, the prosecutor delivered me three shillings, which were all marked, and the next morning about half-past seven o'clock, I laid out two of them at his shop—I gave them to the prisoner—they were both marked alike—this is one of the shillings.
Cross-examined. Q. What became of the other shilling? A. I have left it at home—I gave the prosecutor a shilling for it—I had left the prosecutor's shop before the policeman was called in—there was one woman inthe shop when I was there—I purchased a piece, of bacon.
JAMES WILLIAM BURRIDGE ( police-constable V 160.) I searched the prisoner, and found the tea—this shilling he produced himself, I believe from his coat pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. How many shillings had he? A. Three, and one half-crown, besides this shilling—he took them all out, and his master looked over them, and found the marked shilling among them—this is it—he made no difficulty in producing his money.
(The prisoner received a goad character.)
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18390408-1462]
1462. ANN M'DERMOT was indicted for, that one Pamela Elizabeth Hall, on the 13th of November, did steal 2 half-crowns, 6 shillings, and 3 sixpences, the monies of James Fagg; and that the said Ann M'Dermot did feloniously and maliciously incite, mote, procure, counsel, hire and command the said Pamela Elisabeth Hall the felony aforesaid to do and commit; against the Statute, &c.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
PAMELA ELIZABETH HALL . I was fourteen yean old on the 16th of last October, and live with my grandmother, Mrs. Fagg, at No. 9, Bermondsey-street. I was living with her in November last—the prisoner. had at one time lodged in my grandmother's house—I have taken half-crowns, shillings, and sixpences belonging to my grandmother a great many times by desire of the prisoner—in November I took two half-crowns, six shillings, and three sixpences, by her desire—I was in the prisoner's house when she desired me to do it—I went to her house because she told me I was to go there—great part of the money I took was spent in drink, and the other part bought furniture—I bought some at Mr. Smith's at Crucifix-lane, and at Snow's-fields—I bought some chain and some fire-irons with the money I took from my grandmother, and they were taken to the prisoner's house—the prisoner was with me when. I bought some of the things, and her daughter was with me when I bought some of them—the prisoner told me that John Hubbard was to marry me—the chain that were produced from Mr. Warton's, the pawnbroker, were the same that were bought at Mr. Smith's—some china cups and saucers were bought with my grandmother's money, the prisoner's daughter went with me to buy them—I used frequently to go to the prisoner I in the after-noon, and stop all the afternoon—I slept there two nights—I do not recollect when I told my grandmother of taking the money, but I did tell her—I took the money because the prisoner told me—she told me several times to do it before I did it.
Prisoner. I was forty-three miles down in Kent, and my daughter and John Hubbard, and this girl, kept company for two years, and they had the house to do as they pleased. Witness. She was not in the country, she was at home in November—I did not keep company with her daughter above nine months—I knew John Hubbard before I knew her—the prisoner told me to do this a great many times before I did it—a coat was bought with a sovereign that I took from my grandmother, and the prisoner gave me a bad sovereign to put in its place—I did not go to buy the coat—there had been 2 s. paid off it, and I paid 16 s. at the prisoner's desire, out of the sovereign—the rest of the sovereign was spent in gin and ale at the prisoner's house the same night—the prisoner forced me to drink some.
Prisoner. You know you said you would come to my place when you liked—you knew John Hubbard before you knew me—he said he thought more of my daughter than of you, and then you stated this about the sovereign, and said any thing was your property. Witness. John Hubbard never had any thing to say to her daughter that I know of—what I have said is the truth.
ANN FAGG . I am the wife of James Fagg of Bermondsey-street, a butcher. Pamela Elizabeth Hall was born in my house, find lived with me, except when she went on a visit to my son—the prisoner lodged in my house, and left me on the 16th of October last—both before and after she
was gone, I missed money—I missed money in November, but it is impossible to say to what amount—Hall was in the shop, and took money the same as I did—she behaved with the greatest propriety before the prisoner came, and was liked by every neighbour—I think it is about a month or five weeks ago that Hall first told me about taking any money—I believe I have lost more than 20 l., but Hall lives with me still, and always shall—I will forgive her.
Prisoner. If you lost money, where are Mary Sampson's fine dresses which the girl confessed she bought? Witness. I know her, she is rather older than ray granddaughter—I know John Hubbard—I heard that he and Hall kept company, but I never saw them together—I believe his parents are hard-working people.
THOMAS SMITH . I am a broker, and live in Crucifix-lane, Snow's-fields. In November Hall came to my shop—the prisoner's daughter had come then with her, and the prisoner herself had come with her—Hall bought some fire-irons of me, and to my best recollection, the prisoner's daughter was with her then—I had a table, the prisoner's daughter came for it, and asked the price of it, Hall then came with her, and then the prisoner came with Hall, and looked at it—they did not buy it—the prisoner came with Hall to buy a saucepan, and Hall paid for it—Hall paid for some chain and some pictures.
Prisoner. I never bought a saucepan of you in my life—it was my husband who was after the table a long while—I said it was not worth the money. Witness. At the time you dealt for the saucepan, a surgeon came by, who you said had saved your life.
EDWARD BENNETT . I am in the employ of Mr. Walton, a pawnbroker, I have two chairs, half a dozen cups and saucers, and two dishes which were pawned by the prisoner.
PAMELA ELIZABETH HALL re-examined. I paid for thesechairs.
Prisoner. I pawned the chairs, the cups and saucers, and dishes—my daughter said they were bought with my husband's club-money.
JOHN KINSEY . I am an officer of the Borough. On the night of the 8th of March, I went to the prisoner's house in Stoney-lane—I saw there was a light in the room—the shutters were closed—I knocked at the door—the prisoner said, "Who is there?"—I said, "A friend, open the door"—I knocked again, and said, "Is Mrs. M'Dermot at home?"—the prisoner said, "No, she is not"—I wrenched the shutters open, and was in the act of lifting the window, when the prisoner said, "Put the window down, I will open the door," which she did—I then went in and found this box on the mantel-piece, with the duplicates of some of the property in it—I apprehended her.
Prisoner's Defence. I am quite falsely sworn against—it is all jealousy—I told this girl's grandmother that she was seen with a deal of money.
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18390408-1463]
1463. GEORGE TUCKER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 1 counterpane, value 6 s.; 1 pair of breeches, value 1 s. 6 d., 1 apron, value 19 d.; and 1 flannel shirt, value 19 d.; the goods of Henry Chubb.
HENRY CHUBB . I live at Stockwell, the prisoner lodged at my house. On the 12th of March I missed a counterpane, and the other articles—some of them had been in his room—these are them—( examining them.)
MARK BREAKSPEAR ( police-constable E 30.) On the 12th of March I saw the prisoner with a bundle—I asked what he had got—he said a few things of his own—I found in this apron a shirt and breeches—he said he did not steal them, but took them from his lodging—the counterpane had been pawned.
Prisoner's Defence. I was out of work and in great distress.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18390408-1464]
1464. THOMAS CONNOR, GEORGE BRATT, JAMES SANDS , and HENRY CHURCHILL , were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 150lbs. weight of lead, value 1 l. 5 s. the goods of Daniel Smith, and fixed to a certain building.
HENRY CAVELL . I am a plasterer's labourer. I live opposite Mr. Daniel Smith's warehouse, which is in Woolpack-yard, Kent-street—on the 12th of March, about half-past eleven o'clock in the evening, I was looking out of window, and there were some men below—at twelve o'clock some man went from the bottom room of the house I was in, to the end of the court, and some person went past the court—the man who went from the house came back again, and said, "Now then," and immediately something was thrown off the roof of the warehouse—it was white, and I thought it was a dead body rolled up in a sheet—I called Thomas Dennead to look at it—he got out of bed, in the room where I was, and looked at it—there were then two men on the roof of the warehouse—I saw them, but did not know who they were—after that I saw one man drop from the top of the warehouse, down by the water-closet—he came straight across, and to the room I was in—it was Connor—we asked him what it was he threw off the warehouse—he said about 2 cwt of lead—be then borrowed the key of the young man I was stopping with, and went down stairs to the bottom room of the house I was in.
THOMAS DENNEAD . I live in the same room with Cavell—he called me, I got out of bed, and saw two men crossing the road with the lead, and saw two men on the roof—I saw them come down by the spout, and jump on the water-closet—the lead was brought into the room under mine—Sands was one of the men who brought it, and I think that Bratt was the other, but he has a different dress on now—Bratt and Sands live in a room in the next court—I had known them before—Connor then came to me and borrowed the key—he said he turned down about 2 cwt of lead—I saw lead the next morning under a table in Connor's room.
JANE JOHNSON . I lodge at the house in Woolpack-yard. At two o'clock, on Wednesday, the 13th of March, I went into Connor's room for a light—I saw some lead partly up the chimney, and part in the fire-place—it was black, and I asked if it was leather—Connor said, "Yes"—there was a frying pan under the grate, and the melted lead fell into the pan.
CHARLES BURRIDGE ( police-constable M 77.) I went into Connor's room on that Wednesday, and apprehended him with Bratt and Sands—they were lying on the bed—I found no lead there.
JOHN BLAZER ( police-constable M 145.) I went to Connor's room, and found a small piece of lead on the table, and some melted lead in the fire-place.
DANIEL SMITH . This was my warehouse, and was stripped off its
lead—I had seen it all safe in the afternoon, previous to its being taken—I lost about forty square feet of lead from the front of the warehouse.
JURY to THOMAS DENNEAD. Q. Can you speak to Sands positively? A. Yes, he was one who picked up the lead—I was about thirty yards from him—Connor and Churchill were on the top of the roof and came down by the water-closet—I think Brett was one who picked up the lead.
Connor's Defence, The persons who have come against me have all sworn falsely—the first witness goes about begging with a soldier's jacket on—the other witnesses are the same.
Sands Defence. I know no more about it than a child.
Churchill's Defence. I was not near the place.
CONNOR— GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Seven Years.
SANDS— GUILTY . Aged 42.— Confined Six Months.
BRATT AND CHURCHILL— NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1465. GEORGE JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of March, 1 1/4 pint of brandy, value 5 s. 6 d., 11/2 pint of gin, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of William Coulton, his master: to which he pleaded
GUILTY — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18390408-1466]
1466. LOUISA FERGUSON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ANN CHESNET . I am the wife of Joseph Chesney, a stationer in Bermondsey-street On the morning of the 3rd of March the prisoner came in for a "Paul Pry," which came to 3 d.—she gave me 2 d. for it—I said I could not let her have it—she said she had been in the habit of having it every week for that—I said it had been raised to 3 d. for three months, but perhaps she meant the "Town"—she then said, "Let me have the "Town"—I gave her a "Town," which is 2 d.—she gave me a sixpence, and I gave her 4 d. change—I laid the sixpence on a shelf behind me—after she was gone I looked at it, and thought it was a bad one—I showed it to my daughter—I then put it by itself in a purse, which I have now in my pocket, and threw it into the till—it remained there till the prisoner came—on the 17th of March, when my daughter was in the shop, the prisoner came in, I heard her voice—I came into the shop, and then she wanted a "Paul Pry" for 2 d.—I told her she could not have it for that—she had the "Town" again, and offered a bad sixpence to my daughter, who did not give her change, but kept the sixpence in her hand, and showed it to me—I told the prisoner it was bad, and that she had passed one to me that very day fortnight—she said no, she had not been in the shop before—my husband sent for the policeman, and gave him the two sixpences.
LOUISA CHESNEY . I am the prosecutrix's daughter. On Sunday, the 17th of March, the prisoner came to my mother's shop—she asked for a twopenny "Paul Pry"—I told her it was 3 d.—she then changed to the "Town," and offered me a counterfeit sixpence—my mother came in, and the sixpence was detained—I marked it, and gave it to the policeman.
ROBERT ROSOMAN ( police-constable M 158.) I was called in on the 17th of March—I took the prisoner, and received these two sixpences.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These are both counterfeit.
Prisoner. I acknowledge the sixpence was bad; but I was driven to it by distress asked a young man to lend me a shilling, and he gave me the sixpence to pass—I had not been there before.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18390408-1467]
1467. JOHN HOLBROOK was indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution,
JOSEPH IZOD . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Blackman-street, in the Borough. On the 28th of March the prisoner came to my shop for half a pound of bacon—it came to 4 d.—he gave me a half-crown, which was bad—I told him so, I kept it, and gave it to the officer.
THOMAS READY . On the 29th of March, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to my shop and bought an article, which came to 6 d.—he gave me a half-crown—I suspected it was bad, and kept it by itself—I gave him two shillings change, and he left—the policeman came soon afterwards—I marked the half-crown, and gave it him.
Prisoner. I called for half a pound of boiled beef. Witness. Yes, and I had none—then you had roast beef—you were in the shop about five minutes—another man came in while you were there, who I considered was one of your quality —he tried to attract my attention—I could swear to you.
JOHN BLAZER . I am an officer. I went to Mr. Izod's on the 30th of March—he gave me this half-crown, which I produce.
GEORGB HAVILL ( police-sergeant M 2.) Mr. Ready gave me this half-crown.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These half-crowns are both counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18390408-1468]
1468. LAWRENCE LEE was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of March, 1 ass, price 1 l., the property of John Bradfield; and 1 ass, price 1 l.; the property of Samuel Smith.
JOHN BRADFIELD . I live at Wimbledon. On the morning of the 23rd of March, my boy turned my donkey out, it was afterwards missing, and is worth 1 l. 1 s.—Samuel Smith, my son-in-law, also lost his donkey. I saw them both at Deptford.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. When had you seen it safe? A. On the night before—I saw it again on the Tuesday week following.
HENRY BRADFIELD . I am the prosecutor's son. I turned out a male donkey on Wimbledon-common, on the Saturday morning—it was missed that day—I saw it afterwards at the Fountain, at Deptford.
THOMAS HORRICK . I am a chair-bottomer. On Saturday evening, the 30th of March, Christopher Jones came, and asked me to buy a donkey—he wanted 28 s. for it—I said it was too much—he said he had another in the yard—I said I would look at that—he brought in another, and the prisoner came in with him—I asked the price of that—the prisoner said, 26 s.—I said it was too much, and I offered two sovereigns for the two—they would not take it—I offered two guineas—they would not take it—they went away, and in ten minutes came back, and Jones said, if I would stand beer-money I should have them—I said, "No"—they said I should have them—I counted the money on the table—Jones took it up, and he said to the prisoner, "You know what I have to give you for yours."
Cross-examined. Q. Have you found Jones since? A. No—I believe he has been looked after, but he is out of the way.
EDWARD PALMER . I am a police-constable. I went to apprehend the prisoner—he said, "You want me, Mr. Palmer, I will go with you, but am innocent of the case—Smith gave me the donkeys, and took them to the Fountain, but Smith is not here."
ABRAHAM HOWELL . I saw two men driving three donkeys on Wimbledon-common, on the 23rd, but I cannot swear the prisoner was one.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18390408-1469]
1469. EDWARD BURNS and WILLIAM TILLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 10th of April, 1 pair of half-boots, value 4 s., the goods of John Christopher Woollacot and another; to which Burns pleaded
GUILTY . * Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years.
ELIZABETH GOLDING . I live nearly opposite Mr. Woollacot in Mint-street, Borough. I saw the two prisoners there on the 10th of April, about a quarter before ten o'clock at night—they went into the shop—I saw Bums give a snatch, and the boots fell down—they came out, and Burns returned, and took the boots—they went on towards St. George's church.
WILLIAM HOWLETT . I am in the service of John Christopher Woollacot and another. I was told of this, and missed a pair of half-boots—I went out and pursued Burns, who had got the boots on him.
WILLIAM HUMPHREYS . I am a policeman. I saw the prisoner in company near the window, peeping and looking in several times—I saw Burns snatch the boots.
Tilley's Defence. I had got 100 yards from the place before the robbery was committed, and then Bums ran up to me, and said, "Here they are"—I know nothing about the robbery.
TILLEY— GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Justice Coltman.
